I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n cities connected by m bidirectional roads
2. Each road has a status: working (z=1) or not working (z=0)
3. We need to find a shortest path from city 1 to city n
4. Among all shortest paths, we want to minimize the total number of affected roads
5. Affected roads include:
   - Roads on the path that are not working (must be repaired - z becomes 1)
   - Roads not on the path that are working (must be blown up - z becomes 0)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find the shortest distance from city 1 to city n
We need to compute the shortest path distance using BFS since all edges have equal weight (1).

### Step 2: Find all shortest paths from city 1 to city n
We need to find the actual path(s) that have the minimum distance. Since we want to minimize affected roads, we need to consider which path has the fewest "problematic" edges.

For any edge not on the shortest path, if it's working (z=1), it will be blown up (z=0). For edges on the path, if they're not working (z=0), they need to be repaired (z=1).

### Step 3: For each possible shortest path, calculate the total affected roads
- Count roads on the path that are not working (need repair)
- Count roads NOT on the path that are working (need to blow up)

### Step 4: Choose the path with minimum total affected roads

However, instead of generating all possible shortest paths (which could be exponential), we can use a more efficient approach:

1. First find the shortest distance d from 1 to n
2. Use BFS to find the shortest path from 1 to n
3. But we need to find the path that minimizes the sum of (non-working edges on path) + (working edges not on path)

Actually, let me think again. The key insight is that we want to minimize:
- Repair cost: number of non-working roads on our chosen path
- Blow-up cost: number of working roads not on our chosen path

So for a given path P, the cost is: (number of non-working edges in P) + (number of working edges not in P)

We can solve this by:
1. Find all edges that could be part of a shortest path
2. For each possible shortest path, calculate the cost
3. Pick the one with minimum cost

But with up to 10^5 nodes, we need a smarter approach.

Alternative approach:
1. Use BFS to find the shortest distance from 1 to n
2. Use dynamic programming or state space search to find the path that minimizes our cost function

Actually, let's use a simpler but effective approach:
- Find the shortest distance d
- For each edge, determine if it can be part of a shortest path
- Use BFS to find all possible shortest paths and evaluate each one

But this might be too slow. Let me think of a better approach.

Better approach:
1. Compute shortest distance from 1 to all nodes using BFS
2. Find all paths from 1 to n with length d
3. For each such path, calculate cost = (non-working edges on path) + (working edges not on path)
4. Choose the path with minimum cost

But finding all shortest paths might be expensive. 

Alternative insight: We can use a modified Dijkstra/BFS that keeps track of the total "cost" as we build paths. But since all edges have cost 1, and we only care about the path length and edge states, we can do:

1. Find shortest distance d from 1 to n
2. Use BFS to find paths, but at each step store (node, cost) where cost is the current total cost to reach that node
3. But we need to consider the specific edge states

Actually, let's go with a simpler but correct approach:
- Use BFS to find the shortest distance from 1 to n
- Then, for each node, keep track of the minimum possible cost to reach that node (where cost includes repair + blow-up costs)
- But this is complex because the cost depends on the path taken

Let me reconsider: the blow-up cost is the number of working edges not on the path. This is hard to track in standard BFS.

Alternative approach - since we only need one optimal path, and there might not be many short paths, let's:
1. Find all shortest paths from 1 to n using a simple BFS that tracks paths
2. For each path, calculate the cost
3. Pick the one with minimum cost

For efficiency, I'll use BFS with state = (current_node, parent) to reconstruct paths.

But worst case, there could be many shortest paths.

Another insight: the total cost for a path P is:
- Repair cost: sum over edges e in P of (1 if e is not working else 0)
- Blow-up cost: sum over edges e not in P of (1 if e is working else 0)

This can be rewritten as:
Total cost = (sum of non-working edges in P) + (sum of working edges not in P)

Let me denote:
- Total working edges in the graph = W
- Working edges in path P = W_P
- Then working edges not in P = W - W_P
- Non-working edges in P = (total edges in P) - W_P
- So total cost = (total edges in P - W_P) + (W - W_P) = (total edges in P) + W - 2*W_P

Since total edges in P is the same for all shortest paths (it's the shortest distance d), we get:
Total cost = d + W - 2*W_P

To minimize cost, we need to maximize W_P, i.e., maximize the number of working edges in the path.

Therefore, among all shortest paths, we should pick the one that has the maximum number of working edges.

This is a crucial optimization!

So the algorithm becomes:
1. Find the shortest distance d from 1 to n using BFS
2. Among all shortest paths from 1 to n, find the one with maximum working edges (W_P)
3. For that path, compute the affected roads:
   - For each edge on the path: if it's not working, mark it for repair (z becomes 1)
   - For each edge not on the path: if it's working, mark it for blow-up (z becomes 0)

Now I just need to find a shortest path from 1 to n with maximum working edges.

This can be solved with a modified BFS or Dijkstra:
- State: (node, current_working_edges_count)
- We want to maximize working edges count while minimizing path length (but since all shortest paths have same length, we only care about maximizing working edges)

Actually, since we're constrained to shortest paths, I can first find all nodes at distance k from start, then do a DFS/BFS on those nodes to find path with max working edges.

Algorithm:
1. Use BFS to find shortest distance d from 1 to n
2. Use BFS to find all nodes at distance k from 1 for k = 0,1,...,d
3. Start from node 1 (distance 0), use BFS that tracks working edge count
4. For each node, track the maximum working edges count achievable at that distance
5. Reconstruct the path

But we need to track both distance and working edge count.

Actually, we can use dynamic programming:
- dp[node][dist] = maximum working edges we can have at node with distance exactly dist from start

But distance is up to n, and nodes up to 10^5, so this might be O(n^2) which is too slow.

Alternative: since we want shortest paths only, we can:
1. Find the shortest distance d from 1 to n
2. Build a graph of only nodes at distance k from 1 for k = 0 to d
3. Do a DFS/BFS on this graph (which is a tree-like structure) to find the path from 1 to n with maximum working edges

But building this intermediate graph might be expensive.

Simpler approach: use a modified BFS that stores for each node the best (max working edges) achievable at that distance. Since the path length is fixed for shortest paths, we can do:

- Use BFS with state (node), and at each node, keep track of the maximum working edges count for any path from 1 to that node with shortest distance
- We update: if we can reach node u from v with a working edge, then working count increases by 1
- Otherwise, same count

But we need to ensure we're only using shortest paths.

Algorithm:
1. Initialize dist[1] = 0, work[1] = 0
2. Use a queue (BFS)
3. For each node, when we visit it, we know the shortest distance and the maximum working edges count achievable
4. Process neighbors: if we can reach neighbor with shorter distance, or same distance with more working edges, update
5. But BFS by level ensures shortest distance

Actually, let's implement a BFS that at each level (distance) finds the maximum working edges count.

```python
    key = (min(x,y), max(x,y), z)
```

```python
from collections import deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())

edges = []
adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
edge_dict = {}  # (min(x,y), max(x,y)) -> z

for _ in range(m):
    x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((x, y, z))
    adj[x].append((y, z))
    adj[y].append((x, z))
    key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
    edge_dict[key] = z

# Find shortest distance from 1 to n
dist = [-1] * (n+1)
dist[1] = 0
queue = deque([1])

while queue:
    u = queue.popleft()
    for v, z in adj[u]:
        if dist[v] == -1:
            dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
            queue.append(v)

# If dist[n] is -1, but problem says connected, so it's reachable
# Now find a path from 1 to n with maximum working edges
# Use BFS with state (node, current_working_count)
# But we want shortest path first, then maximize working edges
# So we process by distance level

# First, group nodes by distance
nodes_by_dist = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
for i in range(1, n+1):
    if dist[i] != -1:
        nodes_by_dist[dist[i]].append(i)

# work_count[node] = maximum working edges in any shortest path to node
work_count = [-1] * (n+1)
work_count[1] = 0

# For each distance level, update work_count
for d in range(n):
    if d > dist[n]:
        break
    for u in nodes_by_dist[d]:
        if work_count[u] == -1:
            continue
        for v, z in adj[u]:
            if dist[v] == d + 1:
                new_work = work_count[u] + (1 if z == 1 else 0)
                if work_count[v] < new_work:
                    work_count[v] = new_work

# Now find a specific path that achieves work_count[n]
# Reconstruct path using parent tracking
parent = [-1] * (n+1)
parent_edge = [-1] * (n+1)  # (parent_node, edge_status)

# Use BFS to find such a path
# For each node, we want to know the best path to reach it
# We'll store for each node, the parent and edge status
# We can do BFS in order of increasing distance
work_count_copy = work_count[:]
work_count_copy[1] = 0

# Instead, let's do a BFS that only considers edges that are in shortest path
# Define edge is in shortest path if dist[u] + 1 == dist[v] or dist[v] + 1 == dist[u]
from collections import defaultdict

short_adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
for u in range(1, n+1):
    for v, z in adj[u]:
        if dist[u] + 1 == dist[v] or dist[v] + 1 == dist[u]:
            short_adj[u].append((v, z))

# Now find a path from 1 to n with maximum working edges
# Use BFS with work_count
work_count = [-1] * (n+1)
work_count[1] = 0
parent = [-1] * (n+1)
parent_edge = [-1] * (n+1)  # (from_node, edge_status)

queue = deque([1])
while queue:
    u = queue.popleft()
    for v, z in short_adj[u]:
        if work_count[v] < work_count[u] + (1 if z == 1 else 0):
            work_count[v] = work_count[u] + (1 if z == 1 else 0)
            parent[v] = u
            parent_edge[v] = (u, z)
            queue.append(v)

# Reconstruct path

current = n
while current != 1:

    path_edges.append((u, current, z))
    current = u
path_edges.reverse()

# Now find affected roads
affected = []

# First, for each edge on the path that is not working (z=0), repair it (to 1)
for u, v, z in path_edges:
    if z == 0:
        affected.append((u, v, 1))

# Then, for each edge in the original graph that is not on this path and is working (z=1), blow it up (to 0)
# We need to represent edges in canonical form
edge_set_path = set()
for u, v, z in path_edges:
    edge_set_path.add((min(u, v), max(u, v), z))

# For each original edge
for x, y, z in edges:
    key = (min(x, y), max(x, y), z)
    # If this edge is not on the path and is working
    if key not in edge_set_path and z == 1:
        affected.append((x, y, 0))

# Output
```

- Path: 1-2, z=0, so need repair: (1,2,1)
- Output: 1\n1 2 1 ✓

Example 2: n=4, m=4, edges:
1-2:1, 1-3:0, 2-3:1, 3-4:1
- What is shortest path to 4? 1-3-4 has length 2, 1-2-3-4 has length 3, so shortest length is 2
- Path 1-3-4: edge 1-3 (z=0), 3-4 (z=1)
- Working edges in path: only 3-4 (1 working edge)
- Non-working edges on path: 1-3 (z=0) -> need repair
- Other working edges: 1-2 (z=1) is not on path, so need blow up
- Other working edges: 2-3 (z=1) is not on path, so need blow up
- So blow up 1-2 and 2-3, repair 1-3
- Affected: (1,3,1), (1,2,0), (2,3,0)
- But expected output is: 3\n1 2 0\n1 3 1\n2 3 0 ✓

Wait, expected output is 1 2 0, 1 3 1, 2 3 0
Yes! Matches.

In our calculation: we have to blow up (1,2,0) -> becomes (1,2,0) but wait - it's already working? No, z=1 in input.

Input: 1-2:1, so working. We blow it up to 0. 1-3:0, we repair to 1. 2-3:1, we blow up to 0. So: (1,2,0), (1,3,1), (2,3,0) ✓

So the code should be correct.

```python
from collections import deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())

edges = []
adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
edge_dict = {}  # key: (min(u,v), max(u,v)), value: z

for _ in range(m):
    x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((x, y, z))
    adj[x].append((y, z))
    adj[y].append((x, z))
    key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
    edge_dict[key] = z

# Find shortest distance from 1 to n
dist = [-1] * (n+1)
dist[1] = 0
queue = deque([1])

while queue:
    u = queue.popleft()
    for v, z in adj[u]:
        if dist[v] == -1:
            dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
            queue.append(v)

# Find a path from 1 to n with maximum working edges
# Use BFS on shortest path edges only
short_adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
for u in range(1, n+1):
    for v, z in adj[u]:
        if dist[u] + 1 == dist[v] or dist[v] + 1 == dist[u]:
            short_adj[u].append((v, z))

# BFS to find path with maximum working edges
work_count = [-1] * (n+1)
work_count[1] = 0
parent = [-1] * (n+1)
parent_edge = [-1] * (n+1)  # (from_node, edge_status)

queue = deque([1])
while queue:
    u = queue.popleft()
    for v, z in short_adj[u]:
        new_work = work_count[u] + (1 if z == 1 else 0)
        if work_count[v] < new_work:
            work_count[v] = new_work
            parent[v] = u
            parent_edge[v] = (u, z)
            queue.append(v)

# Reconstruct path
path_edges = []
current = n
while current != 1:
    u, z = parent_edge[current]
    path_edges.append((u, current, z))
    current = u
path_edges.reverse()

# Find affected roads
affected = []

# For each edge on path that is not working, repair it
for u, v, z in path_edges:
    if z == 0:
        affected.append((u, v, 1))

# For each edge not on path that is working, blow it up
# Create set of edges on path with canonical form
path_set = set()
for u, v, z in path_edges:
    path_set.add((min(u, v), max(u, v), z))

for x, y, z in edges:
    key = (min(x, y), max(x, y), z)
    if key not in path_set and z == 1:
        affected.append((x, y, 0))

print(len(affected))
for u, v, z in affected:
    print(u, v, z)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Breaking Good is a new video game which a lot of gamers want to have. There is a certain level in the game that is really difficult even for experienced gamers.

Walter William, the main character of the game, wants to join a gang called Los Hermanos (The Brothers). The gang controls the whole country which consists of n cities with m bidirectional roads connecting them. There is no road is connecting a city to itself and for any two cities there is at most one road between them. The country is connected, in the other words, it is possible to reach any city from any other city using the given roads. 

The roads aren't all working. There are some roads which need some more work to be performed to be completely functioning.

The gang is going to rob a bank! The bank is located in city 1. As usual, the hardest part is to escape to their headquarters where the police can't get them. The gang's headquarters is in city n. To gain the gang's trust, Walter is in charge of this operation, so he came up with a smart plan.

First of all the path which they are going to use on their way back from city 1 to their headquarters n must be as short as possible, since it is important to finish operation as fast as possible.

Then, gang has to blow up all other roads in country that don't lay on this path, in order to prevent any police reinforcements. In case of non-working road, they don't have to blow up it as it is already malfunctional. 

If the chosen path has some roads that doesn't work they'll have to repair those roads before the operation.

Walter discovered that there was a lot of paths that satisfied the condition of being shortest possible so he decided to choose among them a path that minimizes the total number of affected roads (both roads that have to be blown up and roads to be repaired).

Can you help Walter complete his task and gain the gang's trust?


-----Input-----

The first line of input contains two integers n, m (2 ≤ n ≤ 10^5, $0 \leq m \leq \operatorname{min}(\frac{n(n - 1)}{2}, 10^{5})$), the number of cities and number of roads respectively.

-----Output-----

In the first line output one integer k, the minimum possible number of roads affected by gang.

In the following k lines output three integers describing roads that should be affected. Each line should contain three integers x, y, z (1 ≤ x, y ≤ n, $z \in \{0,1 \}$), cities connected by a road and the new state of a road. z = 1 indicates that the road between cities x and y should be repaired and z = 0 means that road should be blown up. 

You may output roads in any order. Each affected road should appear exactly once. You may output cities connected by a single road in any order. If you output a road, it's original state should be different from z.

After performing all operations accroding to your plan, there should remain working only roads lying on some certain shortest past between city 1 and n.

If there are multiple optimal answers output any.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n cities connected by m bidirectional roads
2. Each road has a state: working (z=1) or not working (z=0)
3. We need to find the shortest path from city 1 to city n
4. Among all shortest paths, we want to minimize the total number of affected roads
5. Affected roads include:
   - Roads on the path that are not working (need to be repaired - set to 1)
   - Roads not on the path that are working (need to be blown up - set to 0)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find the shortest distance from city 1 to city n
- Use BFS since all edges have equal weight (1)
- We need to find the shortest path length from 1 to n

### Step 2: Find all shortest paths from 1 to n
- We need to find all paths that have the minimum distance
- For each node, we'll keep track of the shortest distance and the predecessors

### Step 3: For each shortest path, calculate the cost
- Cost = (number of non-working roads on the path) + (number of working roads not on the path)
- We want to minimize this cost

### Step 4: Choose the optimal path and output the affected roads

Let me implement this:

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict
import sys

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Store roads
    roads = []
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    road_dict = {}  # (x, y) -> z, (x,y) and (y,x) are the same
    
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
        roads.append((x, y, z))
        graph[x].append((y, z))
        graph[y].append((x, z))
        road_dict[(min(x, y), max(x, y))] = z
    
    # If n == 1, then we're already at destination
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    dist[1] = 0
    queue = deque([1])
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
            if dist[neighbor] == -1:
                dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # If no path exists (shouldn't happen as problem states country is connected)
    if dist[n] == -1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Find all shortest paths from 1 to n
    # We'll use BFS to find the optimal path
    # For each node, store the predecessors that lead to shortest path
    
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    # We need to find all possible shortest paths, so we'll do BFS and track all possible parents
    
    # Better approach: do BFS and build a tree of shortest paths
    # Keep track of which nodes can reach n in minimum distance
    reachable = [False] * (n + 1)
    reachable[1] = True
    queue = deque([1])
    min_dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    min_dist[1] = 0
    parent_list = defaultdict(list)  # node -> list of parent nodes
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
            if min_dist[neighbor] == -1 or min_dist[neighbor] > min_dist[node] + 1:
                min_dist[neighbor] = min_dist[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
                parent_list[neighbor].append(node)
    
    # Now we have all paths that can reach n in min_dist[n]
    # We need to find one path (or multiple) that minimizes affected roads
    
    # First, let's find a specific path from 1 to n that minimizes total affected roads
    # Affected roads cost:
    # 1. For roads on the path: if road is not working (z=0), we need to repair it (cost +1)
    # 2. For roads not on the path: if road is working (z=1), we need to blow it up (cost +1)
    
    # We'll use BFS to find the optimal path with minimum cost
    # But we can't easily try all paths due to constraints
    
    # Alternative approach: since we want shortest path, and among those we want minimum affected roads
    # We can do: for each node in shortest path, the cost depends on the specific path
    # We can do a BFS that keeps track of the cost to reach each node
    
    # Let's build the actual shortest path from 1 to n
    # We'll try to find one path that minimizes the cost
    
    # First, let's find the total number of working roads not on the path
    # To minimize cost, we want to avoid having working roads not on the path
    
    # Actually, we need to consider that for a fixed path, the cost is:
    # cost = (sum of z_values for edges on path where z=0) + (sum of z_values for edges not on path where z=1)
    
    # We can use Dijkstra-like approach with state (node, path_cost) but it's too complex
    
    # Simpler: since all edges have weight 1 for path length, we can try to build all possible shortest paths
    # and compute the cost for each one
    
    # But with n up to 10^5, we can't enumerate all paths
    
    # Alternative insight: we want to minimize cost = sum of "bad" edges on path + sum of "good" edges not on path
    # This is equivalent to: total_good_edges - sum of good edges on path + sum of bad edges on path
    # = total_good_edges + sum of (bad_edges_on_path - good_edges_on_path)
    # = total_good_edges + sum of (0 - 1) for bad edges on path + sum of (1 - 1) for good edges on path
    # = total_good_edges - sum of 1 for each bad edge on path
    # = total_good_edges - (number of bad edges on path)
    
    # So minimizing cost is equivalent to maximizing (number of good edges on path)
    
    # Therefore, we should choose the shortest path that contains the maximum number of working roads!
    
    # This is a key insight!
    
    # So our algorithm becomes:
    # 1. Find shortest path from 1 to n
    # 2. Among all shortest paths, find the one with maximum number of working roads
    # 3. For that path, we need to:
    #    - Repair all roads on the path that are not working (z=0 -> z=1)
    #    - Blow up all working roads not on the path (z=1 -> z=0)
    
    # Now we need to find the shortest path with maximum working edges
    
    # We can use modified Dijkstra or BFS with state being (node, num_good_edges)
    # But we want to find the maximum possible good edges among all shortest paths
    
    # We can do a BFS that tracks the distance and the number of good edges
    # Actually, we can do a two-step process:
    # 1. First find shortest distance d from 1 to n
    # 2. Then find a path of length d that maximizes the sum of good edges on it
    
    # We'll do a BFS that tracks (node, good_edges_so_far) but that might be too expensive
    
    # Alternative: we can use dynamic programming on BFS layers
    # Layer k contains all nodes at distance k from 1
    
    # Steps:
    # 1. Find shortest distance to each node
    # 2. Build the layers: layers[distance] = set of nodes at that distance
    # 3. For each node, we store the maximum good edges we can get from 1 to that node with shortest path
    # 4. We can do this with BFS from 1
    
    # Initialize
    max_good_edges = [-1] * (n + 1)
    max_good_edges[1] = 0  # at node 1, 0 good edges (none)
    
    # We'll use BFS level by level
    queue = deque([1])
    current_dist = 0
    
    # Store the current layer
    layers = defaultdict(list)
    layers[0] = [1]
    
    # BFS to build layers and track max good edges
    while queue:
        size = len(queue)
        curr_layer = []
        for _ in range(size):
            node = queue.popleft()
            curr_layer.append(node)
        
        # Process all nodes in current layer
        next_layer = []
        current_dist += 1
        
        # For each node in current layer, look at neighbors
        for node in curr_layer:
            for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
                # If neighbor is at distance current_dist + 1, we can reach it
                if dist[neighbor] == current_dist + 1:
                    # If we haven't seen this neighbor or we can get better good edges
                    # But we need to update only when we reach a new layer
                    next_layer.append(neighbor)
                    # For this neighbor, we can update max_good_edges
                    # From node, we get good_edges = max_good_edges[node] + (1 if z == 1 else 0)
                    if max_good_edges[neighbor] < max_good_edges[node] + (1 if z == 1 else 0):
                        max_good_edges[neighbor] = max_good_edges[node] + (1 if z == 1 else 0)
        
        # Add next layer to queue if not empty
        if next_layer:
            queue.extend(next_layer)
            layers[current_dist + 1] = next_layer
    
    # Now we have max_good_edges[n] - the maximum number of working roads on any shortest path
    # But we also need to reconstruct the actual path that achieves this maximum
    
    # We need to find the actual path from 1 to n with maximum good edges
    # Let's trace back from n to 1
    
    # First, let's create parent pointers for the optimal path
    parent_of_path = [-1] * (n + 1)
    # We'll do this in reverse BFS from n to 1
    # We'll add all nodes at distance d to a queue for each d
    
    # Actually, we need to reconstruct a specific path with maximum good edges
    # We'll do a BFS from 1 to n and for each node store the best path to it
    
    # Let's recalculate with parent pointers
    best_parent = [-1] * (n + 1)  # best_parent[i] = the node that leads to maximum good edges to i
    # We already have max_good_edges, now we'll build the path
    
    # Reinitialize and do BFS again but with parent tracking
    max_good_edges = [-1] * (n + 1)
    max_good_edges[1] = 0
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    
    queue = deque([1])
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
            # Check if neighbor is in shortest path (same distance)
            if dist[neighbor] == dist[node] + 1:
                # Calculate good edges from node to neighbor
                good_from_node = max_good_edges[node] + (1 if z == 1 else 0)
                if max_good_edges[neighbor] < good_from_node:
                    max_good_edges[neighbor] = good_from_node
                    parent[neighbor] = node
                    queue.append(neighbor)
    
    # Now we have a path from 1 to n with maximum working roads
    # Reconstruct the path
    path_nodes = []
    current = n
    while current != -1:
        path_nodes.append(current)
        current = parent[current]
    path_nodes.reverse()
    
    # Now we have the optimal path, let's determine which roads to affect
    affected_roads = []
    
    # Step 1: For each road on the path, if it's not working, we need to repair it (z=0 -> z=1)
    # Step 2: For each road not on the path, if it's working, we need to blow it up (z=1 -> z=0)
    
    # First, identify all edges on the path
    path_edges = set()
    path_set = set(path_nodes)
    
    # Build the set of edges in the path
    for i in range(len(path_nodes) - 1):
        u, v = path_nodes[i], path_nodes[i + 1]
        edge_key = (min(u, v), max(u, v))
        path_edges.add(edge_key)
    
    # For each road in input, check if it's on the path or not
    for x, y, z in roads:
        edge_key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
        
        if edge_key in path_edges:
            # This road is on the path
            if z == 0:
                # Need to repair it
                affected_roads.append((x, y, 1))
        else:
            # This road is not on the path
            if z == 1:
                # Need to blow it up
                affected_roads.append((x, y, 0))
    
    # Output the result
    print(len(affected_roads))
    for road in affected_roads:
        print(road[0], road[1], road[2])

solve()
```

Wait, I think there's an issue with my BFS for reconstructing the maximum good edges path. Let me reconsider.

The issue is that when I do the BFS, I might be missing some paths because I only update when I find a better good edges count, but there might be multiple ways to reach a node with the same distance that gives the same good edges count.

Actually, my algorithm should work because I'm updating whenever I find a better (larger) number of good edges.

- Repair road (1,3) from 0 to 1 (because it's on path and not working)
- Blow up road (1,2) from 1 to 0 (not on path and working)
- Blow up road (2,3) from 1 to 0 (not on path and working)

My algorithm finds the path with maximum good edges, and then computes the cost as above.

The cost calculation is correct: cost = (bad edges on path) + (good edges not on path)
= (non-working edges on path) + (working edges not on path)

In the example, path 1-3-4 has one bad edge (1,3 with z=0), and two good edges not on path: (1,2) and (2,3).

Let me fix the code to ensure it handles the parent reconstruction correctly:

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    roads = []
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    road_dict = {}  # (min(x,y), max(x,y)) -> z
    
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
        roads.append((x, y, z))
        graph[x].append((y, z))
        graph[y].append((x, z))
        road_dict[(min(x, y), max(x, y))] = z
    
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Find shortest distance from 1 to all nodes
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    dist[1] = 0
    queue = deque([1])
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
            if dist[neighbor] == -1:
                dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    if dist[n] == -1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Find the path from 1 to n with maximum number of working roads (maximum good edges)
    # Use BFS with state (node, good_edges_so_far)
    # But we want to track only shortest path, so we only consider nodes at distance dist[node]
    
    max_good_edges = [-1] * (n + 1)
    max_good_edges[1] = 0
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)  # parent[i] will store the parent in the optimal path to i
    
    # BFS level by level
    queue = deque([1])
    current_dist = 0
    
    # Process all nodes at distance 0
    while queue:
        size = len(queue)
        next_queue = deque()
        
        for _ in range(size):
            node = queue.popleft()
            
            for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
                # Only consider if neighbor is at exactly current_dist + 1
                if dist[neighbor] == current_dist + 1:
                    # Good edges from node to neighbor: 1 if z==1 else 0
                    good_from_node = max_good_edges[node] + (1 if z == 1 else 0)
                    
                    # If we haven't seen this neighbor or we found better good edges
                    if max_good_edges[neighbor] < good_from_node:
                        max_good_edges[neighbor] = good_from_node
                        parent[neighbor] = node
                        next_queue.append(neighbor)
        
        current_dist += 1
        queue = next_queue
    
    # Reconstruct path from n to 1
    path_nodes = []
    current = n
    while current != -1:
        path_nodes.append(current)
        current = parent[current]
    path_nodes.reverse()
    
    # Identify edges on the path
    path_edges = set()
    for i in range(len(path_nodes) - 1):
        u, v = path_nodes[i], path_nodes[i + 1]
        path_edges.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Determine affected roads
    affected_roads = []
    
    for x, y, z in roads:
        edge_key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
        
        if edge_key in path_edges:
            # Road on path - if not working, need to repair (z=0 -> 1)
            if z == 0:
                affected_roads.append((x, y, 1))
        else:
            # Road not on path - if working, need to blow up (z=1 -> 0)
            if z == 1:
                affected_roads.append((x, y, 0))
    
    print(len(affected_roads))
    for road in affected_roads:
        print(road[0], road[1], road[2])

solve()
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    roads = []
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    road_dict = {}  # (min(x,y), max(x,y)) -> z
    
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
        roads.append((x, y, z))
        graph[x].append((y, z))
        graph[y].append((x, z))
        road_dict[(min(x, y), max(x, y))] = z
    
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Find shortest distance from 1 to all nodes
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    dist[1] = 0
    queue = deque([1])
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
            if dist[neighbor] == -1:
                dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    if dist[n] == -1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Find the path from 1 to n with maximum number of working roads (maximum good edges)
    # Use BFS with state (node, good_edges_so_far)
    # But we want to track only shortest path, so we only consider nodes at distance dist[node]
    
    max_good_edges = [-1] * (n + 1)
    max_good_edges[1] = 0
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)  # parent[i] will store the parent in the optimal path to i
    
    # BFS level by level
    queue = deque([1])
    current_dist = 0
    
    # Process all nodes at distance 0
    while queue:
        size = len(queue)
        next_queue = deque()
        
        for _ in range(size):
            node = queue.popleft()
            
            for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
                # Only consider if neighbor is at exactly current_dist + 1
                if dist[neighbor] == current_dist + 1:
                    # Good edges from node to neighbor: 1 if z==1 else 0
                    good_from_node = max_good_edges[node] + (1 if z == 1 else 0)
                    
                    # If we haven't seen this neighbor or we found better good edges
                    if max_good_edges[neighbor] < good_from_node:
                        max_good_edges[neighbor] = good_from_node
                        parent[neighbor] = node
                        next_queue.append(neighbor)
        
        current_dist += 1
        queue = next_queue
    
    # Reconstruct path from n to 1
    path_nodes = []
    current = n
    while current != -1:
        path_nodes.append(current)
        current = parent[current]
    path_nodes.reverse()
    
    # Identify edges on the path
    path_edges = set()
    for i in range(len(path_nodes) - 1):
        u, v = path_nodes[i], path_nodes[i + 1]
        path_edges.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Determine affected roads
    affected_roads = []
    
    for x, y, z in roads:
        edge_key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
        
        if edge_key in path_edges:
            # Road on path - if not working, need to repair (z=0 -> 1)
            if z == 0:
                affected_roads.append((x, y, 1))
        else:
            # Road not on path - if working, need to blow up (z=1 -> 0)
            if z == 1:
                affected_roads.append((x, y, 0))
    
    print(len(affected_roads))
    for road in affected_roads:
        print(road[0], road[1], road[2])

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Breaking Good is a new video game which a lot of gamers want to have. There is a certain level in the game that is really difficult even for experienced gamers.

Walter William, the main character of the game, wants to join a gang called Los Hermanos (The Brothers). The gang controls the whole country which consists of n cities with m bidirectional roads connecting them. There is no road is connecting a city to itself and for any two cities there is at most one road between them. The country is connected, in the other words, it is possible to reach any city from any other city using the given roads. 

The roads aren't all working. There are some roads which need some more work to be performed to be completely functioning.

The gang is going to rob a bank! The bank is located in city 1. As usual, the hardest part is to escape to their headquarters where the police can't get them. The gang's headquarters is in city n. To gain the gang's trust, Walter is in charge of this operation, so he came up with a smart plan.

First of all the path which they are going to use on their way back from city 1 to their headquarters n must be as short as possible, since it is important to finish operation as fast as possible.

Then, gang has to blow up all other roads in country that don't lay on this path, in order to prevent any police reinforcements. In case of non-working road, they don't have to blow up it as it is already malfunctional. 

If the chosen path has some roads that doesn't work they'll have to repair those roads before the operation.

Walter discovered that there was a lot of paths that satisfied the condition of being shortest possible so he decided to choose among them a path that minimizes the total number of affected roads (both roads that have to be blown up and roads to be repaired).

Can you help Walter complete his task and gain the gang's trust?

The first line of input contains two integers n, m (2 ≤ n ≤ 10^5, $0 \leq m \leq \operatorname{min}(\frac{n(n - 1)}{2}, 10^{5})$), the number of cities and number of roads respectively.

In the following k lines output three integers describing roads that should be affected. Each line should contain three integers x, y, z (1 ≤ x, y ≤ n, $z \in \{0,1 \}$), cities connected by a road and the new state of a road. z = 1 indicates that the road between cities x and y should be repaired and z = 0 means that road should be blown up. 

You may output roads in any order. Each affected road should appear exactly once. You may output cities connected by a single road in any order. If you output a road, it's original state should be different from z.

After performing all operations accroding to your plan, there should remain working only roads lying on some certain shortest past between city 1 and n.

If there are multiple optimal answers output any.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a connected graph with n cities and m bidirectional roads
2. Each road has a state: 0 (not working) or 1 (working)
3. We need to find a shortest path from city 1 to city n
4. Among all shortest paths, we want to minimize the total number of affected roads (both repairs and blowing up)
5. Affected roads:
   - Roads on the path that don't work → need to be repaired (z becomes 1)
   - Roads not on the path that work → need to be blown up (z becomes 0)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find the shortest distance from city 1 to city n
We need to compute the shortest path distance using BFS since all edges have unit weight.

### Step 2: Find all shortest paths from 1 to n
We need to find all paths that have the minimum distance. For this, we can use BFS to determine the shortest distance, then do another BFS from node 1 to find all nodes at distance d from 1, and then find the distance from those nodes to n.

### Step 3: For each shortest path, calculate the cost (number of affected roads)
For a given path:
- For each road on the path: if it's not working (z=0), we need to repair it (cost +1)
- For each road not on the path: if it's working (z=1), we need to blow it up (cost +1)

### Step 4: Choose the path with minimum cost
Since we want to minimize affected roads, we need to find the path that minimizes the sum of repairs and blows.

However, instead of enumerating all paths, we can use dynamic programming or a more efficient approach.

Actually, let me think of a better approach:

We can:
1. Find the shortest distance d from 1 to n
2. Find the shortest path(s) that minimizes the cost of repairs and destructions
3. The cost for a path is: (number of non-working roads on the path) + (number of working roads not on the path)

Wait, this seems complex. Let me reconsider.

Actually, we can:
1. Run BFS to find shortest distance from 1 to n
2. For each node, store the shortest distance from 1 and from n
3. A path is shortest if for every edge (u,v), dist1[u] + 1 = dist1[v] and dist1[v] + 1 = dist2[v] (where dist2 is from n to v)
4. Then we need to find the path that minimizes: sum over edges on path of (1 if edge is broken otherwise 0) + sum over edges not on path of (1 if edge is working otherwise 0)

But this is still complex.

Alternative approach:
Since we want to minimize affected roads, we can think about it as:
- We want to maximize the number of working roads that are not on the path (so we don't blow them up)
- We want to minimize the number of broken roads on the path (so we don't need to repair them)

So ideally, we want a path that:
1. Has as many working roads as possible
2. Avoids working roads that are not on the shortest path

But actually, the total cost is: (number of broken roads on path) + (number of working roads not on path)

We can try to find the minimal cost path by BFS with state (node, current_cost), but the state space is too large.

Let me reconsider the problem. Looking at the examples:

Example 2: 
Input: 4 cities, edges: (1,2,1), (1,3,0), (2,3,1), (3,4,1)
Shortest path from 1 to 4 is length 2.
Possible paths:
- 1-2-3-4: length 3 (not shortest)
- 1-3-4: length 2 (shortest)
This path has one broken road (1-3 with z=0) → need repair (cost 1)
Non-path working roads: only (2,3) and (1,2) are working and not on path? Wait, (1,2) is working and not on path, (2,3) is working and not on path? 
Actually the path is 1-3-4, so edges on path: (1,3) and (3,4)
- On path: (1,3) is broken → need repair (cost 1)
- Not on path: (1,2) is working → need to be blown up (cost 1), (2,3) is working → need to be blown up (cost 1)
Total cost = 3

The output shows cost 3 with operations: 1-2-0, 1-3-1, 2-3-0
So they are blowing up (1,2) and (2,3), and repairing (1,3)

This matches.

So the algorithm:
1. Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
2. Find all paths of that length from 1 to n
3. For each such path, calculate total cost = (number of broken roads on path) + (number of working roads not on path)
4. Choose the path with minimum cost

But finding all shortest paths in a graph of 10^5 nodes might be expensive.

Alternative: We can use dynamic programming with state (node) and keep track of the minimal cost to reach that node, but we also need the actual path.

Actually, we can modify the BFS to keep track of the best path to each node based on cost, but the cost depends on the path.

Perhaps we can do:
- First run BFS to get shortest distance d from 1 to n
- For each node, we store the shortest distance from 1 and from n
- A valid path is one where every edge (u,v) satisfies dist1[u] + 1 = dist1[v]

Then we can do a BFS or use a modified Dijkstra to find the path with minimum cost.

But the cost function is not standard.

I think a simpler approach might work:
Since the graph is not too large (m <= 10^5), and we only care about shortest paths, we can:
1. Run BFS from node 1 to get dist1[i] for all i
2. Run BFS from node n to get dist2[i] for all i
3. For a path to be shortest, for each edge (u,v), we need dist1[u] + 1 = dist1[v] and dist2[u] + 1 = dist2[v]
4. Now we need to find a shortest path that minimizes cost = (sum of broken edges on path) + (sum of working edges not on path)

We can use a modified Dijkstra where the state is (node) and we track the cost to reach that node (which includes edge costs).

The edge cost for an edge (u,v):
- If the edge is broken (z=0) on the path: cost += 1 (because we need to repair it)
- If the edge is working (z=1) and not on the path: we don't directly pay for it, but when we choose a path, we pay for all working edges not on the path.

This is tricky because the cost of "working edges not on path" depends on the entire path, not just individual edges.

Let me define the total cost of a path P as:
- cost(P) = (number of broken edges on P) + (total number of working edges in the graph - number of working edges on P)

Because the working edges not on P = total working edges - working edges on P.

So cost(P) = (broken edges on P) + (total_working - working_edges_on_P)
= (broken edges on P) + (total_working) - (working edges on P)
= total_working + (broken edges on P - working edges on P)

But broken edges on P = broken_edges_on_P, working edges on P = working_edges_on_P
So broken_edges_on_P - working_edges_on_P = (broken_edges_on_P) - (working_edges_on_P)

Actually, let me define for each edge:
- If edge is working: contributes 0 to "broken edges on P" but contributes -1 to (broken - working)
- If edge is broken: contributes 1 to "broken edges on P" but contributes 0 to (broken - working)

So cost(P) = total_working + (broken_edges_on_P - working_edges_on_P)
= total_working + sum_{e on P} (1 if e broken else -1)
= total_working + sum_{e on P} (1 - 2 * (1 if e working else 0))

So for each edge e on path P, its contribution to cost is: (1 if e broken else -1) + total_working

Wait, no: for each edge e on path, if e is working, it contributes -1 to the sum, if e is broken, it contributes +1.

So cost(P) = total_working + sum_{e in P} (1 if e broken else -1)

So for each edge in the path, if it's broken, we add 1, if it's working, we subtract 1.

Therefore, we want to minimize: total_working + sum_{e in P} (1 if e is broken else -1)

Since total_working is constant for all paths, we want to minimize sum_{e in P} (1 if e is broken else -1), which is equivalent to maximizing sum_{e in P} (-1 if e is broken else 1) = sum_{e in P} (1 - 2 * (1 if e broken else 0))

This means we want to maximize the sum of (1 - 2 * broken) over the path, i.e., we want as many working edges as possible on the path.

So the problem reduces to: find a shortest path from 1 to n that maximizes the number of working edges.

This is because:
- cost(P) = total_working + sum_{e in P} (1 if e broken else -1)
- To minimize cost(P), maximize sum_{e in P} (1 if e broken else -1)

So we can solve it as:
1. Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
2. Find the path (or all paths) of that shortest distance that has maximum number of working edges
3. For such a path, the total cost is total_working + (number of broken edges on path - number of working edges on path)

But actually, since we want to minimize the cost, and cost(P) = total_working + (broken_on_P - working_on_P), and broken_on_P - working_on_P is minimized when working_on_P is maximized relative to broken_on_P, we just want to maximize the number of working edges on the path.

So algorithm:
1. Build graph and read input
2. Use BFS to find shortest distance from 1 to n
3. Use modified Dijkstra/BFS to find shortest path from 1 to n that maximizes the number of working edges
4. For the chosen path, calculate the cost = (number of broken edges on path) + (total working edges - number of working edges on path)
5. Output the affected roads: 
   - For each edge on path with z=0: repair it (z becomes 1)
   - For each edge not on path with z=1: blow it up (z becomes 0)

To find the path that maximizes working edges among shortest paths:
We can do a BFS where each state is (node) and we store the maximum working edges we can achieve to reach that node with shortest distance.

Let me implement this:

```python
from collections import deque
import sys

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Store edges
    edges = []
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    total_working = 0
    
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
        edges.append((x, y, z))
        graph[x].append((y, z))
        graph[y].append((x, z))
        if z == 1:
            total_working += 1
    
    # Step 1: Find shortest distance from 1 to n
    dist1 = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([1])
    dist1[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            if dist1[v] == -1:
                dist1[v] = dist1[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # If no path from 1 to n, but problem states country is connected, so should exist
    if dist1[n] == -1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Step 2: Find the path from 1 to n with maximum working edges among shortest paths
    # We'll use BFS with state (node, current_working_count)
    # But instead, we can do: for each node, store the maximum working edges achievable at that distance
    max_working = [-1] * (n + 1)
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    working_count = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    # Initialize
    max_working[1] = 0
    working_count[1] = 0
    
    # We'll use BFS level by level
    queue = deque([1])
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    visited[1] = True
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        
        # Explore neighbors
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            if dist1[v] == dist1[u] + 1:  # This is a valid edge in a shortest path
                # If we can improve the working count, update
                new_working = working_count[u]
                if z == 1:
                    new_working += 1
                
                if max_working[v] < new_working:
                    max_working[v] = new_working
                    working_count[v] = new_working
                    parent[v] = u
                    if not visited[v]:
                        visited[v] = True
                        queue.append(v)
    
    # Now reconstruct path from n to 1
    path_edges = []
    current = n
    while current != 1:
        prev = parent[current]
        # Find the edge between prev and current
        for x, y, z in edges:
            if (x == prev and y == current) or (x == current and y == prev):
                path_edges.append((prev, current, z))
                break
        current = prev
    
    # Now calculate total cost: broken on path + working not on path
    # Working not on path = total_working - working_on_path
    working_on_path = working_count[n]
    broken_on_path = 0
    for x, y, z in path_edges:
        if z == 0:
            broken_on_path += 1
    
    total_cost = broken_on_path + (total_working - working_on_path)
    
    # Now, we need to output affected roads:
    # 1. For each edge on path with z=0: repair it (z becomes 1)
    # 2. For each edge not on path with z=1: blow it up (z becomes 0)
    
    # First, create a set of edges on the path for quick lookup
    path_set = set()
    for x, y, z in path_edges:
        path_set.add((min(x, y), max(x, y), z))
    
    affected_roads = []
    
    # For each original edge, if it's on path and broken, repair it
    for x, y, z in edges:
        xy = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
        if (xy in path_set):
            # This edge is on path
            if z == 0:
                affected_roads.append((x, y, 1))
        else:
            # This edge is not on path
            if z == 1:
                affected_roads.append((x, y, 0))
    
    print(total_cost)
    for road in affected_roads:
        print(road[0], road[1], road[2])

solve()
```

Wait, there's a bug in the path reconstruction. The parent array and the working_count update might not work correctly because we're updating the working count only when we find a better one, but we need to make sure we're exploring all shortest paths.

Actually, the above BFS might not capture all shortest paths because we're updating only when we find a better working count. But in a graph with unit weights and we're doing BFS level by level, this should work.

But actually, when we do BFS level by level, we visit nodes by increasing distance, so for each node at distance d, we consider all edges from nodes at distance d-1 to it.

The issue is that from a node at distance d-1, we can reach a node at distance d, and we update the working count only if we find a path with more working edges.

Since all edges have unit weight, this should give us the maximum possible working edges for each node at each distance.

However, there's a potential issue: multiple edges between same nodes might exist, but the problem says "for any two cities there is at most one road between them".

So we don't have multiple edges.

But there's another issue: the path reconstruction might not work correctly because we're only storing one parent per node.

Let me fix it:

Actually, we don't need to reconstruct the path from the working count alone. We just need to find one path that maximizes working edges.

The algorithm above should work. Let me test with the examples.

- max_working[2] = 0

- total_working = 1 (but wait, there's only one edge and it's broken, so total_working = 0)
- Actually, initially I set total_working = 0 because z=0

- working_on_path = 0
- broken_on_path = 1

- For node 3: distance 1, working_count = 0
- Then from 2: to 3 (z=1) - distance from 2 to 3 is 2, which is dist1[3]=1+1=2, so valid
- From 2 to 3: working count becomes 1 (from 2) + 1 (edge z=1) = 2

- dist1[3] = 1, dist1[2] = 1, so dist1[2] + 1 = 2 ≠ dist1[3] = 1
- So we only consider edges where dist1[v] == dist1[u] + 1

So from node 1 (dist=0):
- To 2: dist1[2]=1, so valid, so update node 2: working_count = 0 + 1 = 1
- To 3: dist1[3]=1, so valid, so update node 3: working_count = 0 + 0 = 0

From node 2 (dist=1):
- To 3: dist1[3]=1, dist1[2]+1 = 2 ≠ 1, so skip
- To 1: dist1[1]=0, dist1[2]+1=2 ≠ 0, skip

From node 3 (dist=1):
- To 4: dist1[4]=2, dist1[3]+1=2, so valid
- So from 3 to 4: working_count = 0 + 1 (since edge (3,4) has z=1) = 1
- So working_count[4] = 1

So the path we get is 1->3->4, working edges: 0 (from 1-3) + 1 (from 3-4) = 1

But is there a path with more working edges? 
Path 1->2->3->4: distance 3, not shortest. Shortest distance is 2.

Shortest paths: only 1->3->4 (distance 2), and also 1->2->? 2 to 4? No direct edge, and 2 to 3 to 4 would be length 3.

So only one shortest path: 1->3->4.

But wait, is 1->2->3->4 a shortest path? Length 3, but dist1[4]=2, so no.

So only path is 1->3->4, with working edges: (3,4) only, so working_on_path=1.

broken_on_path = 0 (edge (1,3) is broken? Actually (1,3) z=0? Yes, so on path and broken → broken_on_path = 1)

working_on_path = 1
total_working = 3
total_cost = 1 + (3 - 1) = 3

Affected roads:
- On path: (1,3) z=0 → repair to 1 → add (1,3,1)
- Not on path: edges (1,2) z=1, (2,3) z=1 → both should be blown up → add (1,2,0), (2,3,0)

Total 3 roads: (1,2,0), (1,3,1), (2,3,0)

But the expected output is:
3
1 2 0
1 3 1
2 3 0

Perfect match.

So the algorithm works.

For the third example, I'll trust it.

One more thing: when reconstructing the path, I stored the parent, but when there are multiple edges between same nodes, we need to be careful. But there's only one edge.

Also, when finding the edge in the edges list, we need to check properly.

Let me fix the edge lookup:

```python
from collections import deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Store edges
edges = []
graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
total_working = 0

for _ in range(m):
    x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((x, y, z))
    graph[x].append((y, z))
    graph[y].append((x, z))
    if z == 1:
        total_working += 1

# Step 1: Find shortest distance from 1 to n
dist1 = [-1] * (n + 1)
queue = deque([1])
dist1[1] = 0

while queue:
    u = queue.popleft()
    for v, z in graph[u]:
        if dist1[v] == -1:
            dist1[v] = dist1[u] + 1
            queue.append(v)

# If no path from 1 to n, but problem states country is connected, so should exist
if dist1[n] == -1:
    print(0)
    exit()

# Step 2: Find maximum working edges on shortest path from 1 to n
# BFS level by level
max_working = [-1] * (n + 1)
parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
working_count = [0] * (n + 1)

# Initialize
max_working[1] = 0
working_count[1] = 0

# Use BFS to process nodes level by level
queue = deque([1])
visited = [False] * (n + 1)
visited[1] = True

while queue:
    u = queue.popleft()
    
    # Explore neighbors
    for v, z in graph[u]:
        # Check if this edge is in a shortest path
        if dist1[v] == dist1[u] + 1:
            # Calculate new working count
            new_working = working_count[u]
            if z == 1:
                new_working += 1
            
            # Update if we found a better (higher) working count
            if max_working[v] < new_working:
                max_working[v] = new_working
                working_count[v] = new_working
                parent[v] = u
                if not visited[v]:
                    visited[v] = True
                    queue.append(v)

# Reconstruct path from n to 1
path_edges = []
current = n
while current != 1:
    prev = parent[current]
    # Find the edge between prev and current
    for x, y, z in edges:
        if (x == prev and y == current) or (x == current and y == prev):
            path_edges.append((prev, current, z))
            break
    current = prev

# Calculate total cost
working_on_path = working_count[n]
broken_on_path = 0
for x, y, z in path_edges:
    if z == 0:
        broken_on_path += 1

total_cost = broken_on_path + (total_working - working_on_path)

# Now, output affected roads
affected_roads = []

# Create set of edges on path for lookup
path_set = set()
for x, y, z in path_edges:
    path_set.add((min(x, y), max(x, y), z))

for x, y, z in edges:
    xy = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
    if (xy in path_set):
        # This edge is on the path
        if z == 0:
            affected_roads.append((x, y, 1))
    else:
        # This edge is not on the path
        if z == 1:
            affected_roads.append((x, y, 0))

print(total_cost)
for road in affected_roads:
    print(road[0], road[1], road[2])
```

This should work correctly.

Note: I used `exit()` instead of `return` since it's at the top level.

- 4 to 6: edge (4,6,1) - working
- 6 to 8: edge (6,8,0) - broken

  - (8,3,0): broken → skip
  - (2,3,1): working → not on path? Path is 1-4-6-8, so (2,3) not on path → should be blown up → but output has (2,3,0)
  - (1,5,1): working, not on path → should be blown up → output has (1,5,0)
  - (8,7,0): broken → skip
  - (5,7,0): broken → skip
  - (6,8,0): already processed

So we have (2,3,0), (1,5,0), (6,8,1) → exactly 3.

But in the output, it's:

6 8 1

Perfect.

But in our algorithm, when we reconstruct the path, we need to make sure we get the path 1-4-6-8.

We need to check if the BFS with maximum working edges finds this.

- dist1: shortest distances from 1
- 1: 0
- 4: 1 (edge 1-4)
- 5: 1 (edge 1-5)
- 2: 2 (1-2-3 or 1-2 directly? 1-2 exists but z=0, so 1 to 2: only through 1-2 with weight 1, but is there direct edge? Yes, but z=0, so length 1? 
Actually, edge (1,2,0) exists, so 1 to 2: distance 1
Similarly, 1 to 3: 1 to 2 to 3? 1-2 (z=0, length 1), 2-3 (z=1, length 1) → total length 2
Or 1-4-? 

Let me compute shortest distances from 1:
- 1: 0
- 2: 1 (direct edge)
- 3: 2 (1-2-3)
- 4: 1 (1-4)
- 5: 1 (1-5)
- 6: 2 (1-4-6)
- 7: 2 (1-5-7) or (1-8-7)? 1-8: ? No direct edge. 1-8? Not directly. 
Edges: 8-3, 8-7, 6-8
So 1 to 8: 1-4-6-8 → length 3
Or 1-5-7-8? 5-7 is broken, so no
Or 1-2-3-8? 3-8 broken, so no
So only path: 1-4-6-8 → length 3

So dist1[8] = 3

Now we find maximum working edges on a path of length 3.

Path 1-2-3-8: length 3
- 1-2: z=0 → working count += 0
- 2-3: z=1 → working count += 1  
- 3-8: z=0 → working count += 0
- total working on path: 1

Path 1-4-6-8:
- 1-4: z=1 → +1
- 4-6: z=1 → +1
- 6-8: z=0 → +0
- total working on path: 2

Path 1-5-7-8: 5-7 is broken (z=0), so edge 5-7 not working
No other path of length 3.

So path 1-4-6-8 has more working edges (2 > 1), so it's chosen.

Therefore, our algorithm should find it.

The BFS with level-by-level processing will find it because at each level, it updates the working count.

So the code should be correct.

Final code:

```python
from collections import deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Store edges
edges = []
graph = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
total_working = 0

for _ in range(m):
    x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
    edges.append((x, y, z))
    graph[x].append((y, z))
    graph[y].append((x, z))
    if z == 1:
        total_working += 1

# Step 1: Find shortest distance from 1 to n
dist1 = [-1] * (n + 1)
queue = deque([1])
dist1[1] = 0

while queue:
    u = queue.popleft()
    for v, z in graph[u]:
        if dist1[v] == -1:
            dist1[v] = dist1[u] + 1
            queue.append(v)

# If no path from 1 to n, but problem states country is connected, so should exist
if dist1[n] == -1:
    print(0)
    exit()

# Step 2: Find maximum working edges on shortest path from 1 to n
# BFS level by level to maximize working edges
max_working = [-1] * (n + 1)
parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
working_count = [0] * (n + 1)

# Initialize
max_working[1] = 0
working_count[1] = 0

# Use BFS to process nodes level by level
queue = deque([1])
visited = [False] * (n + 1)
visited[1] = True

while queue:
    u = queue.popleft()
    
    # Explore neighbors
    for v, z in graph[u]:
        # Check if this edge is in a shortest path
        if dist1[v] == dist1[u] + 1:
            # Calculate new working count
            new_working = working_count[u]
            if z == 1:
                new_working += 1
            
            # Update if we found a better (higher) working count
            if max_working[v] < new_working:
                max_working[v] = new_working
                working_count[v] = new_working
                parent[v] = u
                if not visited[v]:
                    visited[v] = True
                    queue.append(v)

# Reconstruct path from n to 1
path_edges = []
current = n
while current != 1:
    prev = parent[current]
    # Find the edge between prev and current
    for x, y, z in edges:
        if (x == prev and y == current) or (x == current and y == prev):
            path_edges.append((prev, current, z))
            break
    current = prev

# Calculate total cost
working_on_path = working_count[n]
broken_on_path = 0
for x, y, z in path_edges:
    if z == 0:
        broken_on_path += 1

total_cost = broken_on_path + (total_working - working_on_path)

# Now, output affected roads
affected_roads = []

# Create set of edges on path for lookup
path_set = set()
for x, y, z in path_edges:
    path_set.add((min(x, y), max(x, y), z))

for x, y, z in edges:
    xy = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
    if (xy in path_set):
        # This edge is on the path
        if z == 0:
            affected_roads.append((x, y, 1))
    else:
        # This edge is not on the path
        if z == 1:
            affected_roads.append((x, y, 0))

print(total_cost)
for road in affected_roads:
    print(road[0], road[1], road[2])
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Breaking Good is a new video game which a lot of gamers want to have. There is a certain level in the game that is really difficult even for experienced gamers.

Walter William, the main character of the game, wants to join a gang called Los Hermanos (The Brothers). The gang controls the whole country which consists of n cities with m bidirectional roads connecting them. There is no road is connecting a city to itself and for any two cities there is at most one road between them. The country is connected, in the other words, it is possible to reach any city from any other city using the given roads. 

The roads aren't all working. There are some roads which need some more work to be performed to be completely functioning.

The gang is going to rob a bank! The bank is located in city 1. As usual, the hardest part is to escape to their headquarters where the police can't get them. The gang's headquarters is in city n. To gain the gang's trust, Walter is in charge of this operation, so he came up with a smart plan.

First of all the path which they are going to use on their way back from city 1 to their headquarters n must be as short as possible, since it is important to finish operation as fast as possible.

Then, gang has to blow up all other roads in country that don't lay on this path, in order to prevent any police reinforcements. In case of non-working road, they don't have to blow up it as it is already malfunctional. 

If the chosen path has some roads that doesn't work they'll have to repair those roads before the operation.

Walter discovered that there was a lot of paths that satisfied the condition of being shortest possible so he decided to choose among them a path that minimizes the total number of affected roads (both roads that have to be blown up and roads to be repaired).

Can you help Walter complete his task and gain the gang's trust?


-----Input-----

The first line of input contains two integers n, m (2 ≤ n ≤ 10^5, $0 \leq m \leq \operatorname{min}(\frac{n(n - 1)}{2}, 10^{5})$), the number of cities and number of roads respectively.

In following m lines there are descriptions of roads. Each description consists of three integers x, y, z (1 ≤ x, y ≤ n, $z \in \{0,1 \}$) meaning that there is a road connecting cities number x and y. If z = 1, this road is working, otherwise it is not.


-----Output-----

In the first line output one integer k, the minimum possible number of roads affected by gang.

In the following k lines output three integers describing roads that should be affected. Each line should contain three integers x, y, z (1 ≤ x, y ≤ n, $z \in \{0,1 \}$), cities connected by a road and the new state of a road. z = 1 indicates that the road between cities x and y should be repaired and z = 0 means that road should be blown up. 

You may output roads in any order. Each affected road should appear exactly once. You may output cities connected by a single road in any order. If you output a road, it's original state should be different from z.

After performing all operations accroding to your plan, there should remain working only roads lying on some certain shortest past between city 1 and n.

If there are multiple optimal answers output any.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a country with n cities and m bidirectional roads
2. Roads can be working (z=1) or not working (z=0)
3. We need to find a shortest path from city 1 to city n
4. Among all shortest paths, we want to minimize the total number of affected roads
5. Affected roads include:
   - Roads on the path that are not working (need to be repaired, set to z=1)
   - Roads not on the path that are working (need to be blown up, set to z=0)

## Step-by-step Solution

We need to use BFS since all edges are unweighted (even though roads have working/not working status, the distance is based on existence, not on z value).

### Step 2: Find all shortest paths from 1 to n
We need to find all paths of length equal to the shortest distance. Among these, we want the one that minimizes the total affected roads.

### Step 3: Calculate total affected roads for each candidate path
For each candidate path:
- Count roads that are not working (need repair, +1 each)
- Count working roads that are not on the path (need to be blown up, +1 each)

### Step 4: Choose the optimal path and output the results

We need an efficient DP approach.

I'll use: `dp[node][dist] = maximum (working_count - non_working_count)` for all shortest paths to node at distance dist.

We also need to store parent information to reconstruct path.

Steps:
1. Build graph
2. Find shortest distance D from 1 to n using BFS
3. Initialize dp[1][0] = 0
4. For each distance from 0 to D:
   - For each node at current distance, consider all neighbors
   - If neighbor is at distance current_distance + 1, update dp[neighbor][current_distance + 1]
5. Backtrack from n to find path with maximum (working - non_working)

But actually, we need to store the actual path or at least the sequence of edges.

We can store parent information: parent[node][dist] = (parent_node, edge_info) where edge_info contains the edge and its type.

But storing edge info might be complex.

Alternative: store the best profit at each (node, dist), and also store the parent node and the edge added.

Let me implement the BFS with DP:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    total_working = 0
    
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
        graph[x].append((y, z))
        graph[y].append((x, z))
        edges.append((x, y, z))
        if z == 1:
            total_working += 1
    
    # Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    dist[1] = 0
    q = deque([1])
    
    while q:
        u = q.popleft()
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            if dist[v] == -1:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                q.append(v)
    
    # If no path to n
    if dist[n] == -1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Now find a shortest path that minimizes the cost
    # Cost = non_working_on_path + (total_working - working_on_path)
    # = total_working + (non_working_on_path - working_on_path)
    # So we want to minimize (non_working_on_path - working_on_path)
    # Which is equivalent to maximizing (working_on_path - non_working_on_path)
    
    # dp[node][dist] = maximum (working_count - non_working_count) to reach node at distance dist
    # We'll use a dictionary: dp[node] is a dict: distance -> max_profit
    dp = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    parent = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(list))  # parent[node][dist] = (prev_node, edge_type) or None
    
    # Initialize
    dp[1][0] = 0
    
    # BFS by distance
    # We can do level-by-level
    q = deque([(1, 0)])
    
    while q:
        u, d = q.popleft()
        
        # If we already processed this (u,d), skip (though BFS should visit each level once)
        # Process all neighbors
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            new_d = d + 1
            if new_d <= dist[n] and new_d > dist[v]:  # if we can reach v with shorter path, skip
                continue
            if new_d == dist[v]:  # we can reach v with same distance
                # Calculate new profit: (working_count - non_working_count)
                # When we add edge (u,v,z), if z=1, working increases by 1, non_working unchanged
                # If z=0, non_working increases by 1, working unchanged
                # So profit change: if z=1: +1, if z=0: -1
                profit = dp[u][d] + (1 if z == 1 else -1)
                if profit > dp[v][new_d]:
                    dp[v][new_d] = profit
                    parent[v][new_d] = (u, z)
                    q.append((v, new_d))
    
    # Now backtrack from n to 1
    path_nodes = []
    cur_d = dist[n]
    cur = n
    
    while cur != 1:
        prev, z = parent[cur][cur_d]
        path_nodes.append((cur, prev, z))
        cur = prev
        cur_d -= 1
    
    path_nodes.reverse()
    
    # Now we have a path from 1 to n with maximum (working - non_working)
    # But we need to find the actual roads that are on this path
    
    # Get the set of edges on the path
    path_edges = set()
    for u, v, z in path_nodes:
        path_edges.add((min(u, v), max(u, v), z))
    
    # Now calculate total affected roads
    # 1. Roads on path that are not working (need repair)
    # 2. Roads not on path that are working (need to be blown up)
    
    affected_roads = []
    
    # First, for each edge on path, if it's not working, we need to repair it
    for u, v, z in path_nodes:
        if z == 0:  # not working, needs to be repaired
            affected_roads.append((u, v, 1))
    
    # Second, for each edge in original graph that is working but not on path, blow it up
    # First, find all working edges in the original graph
    working_edges = []
    for x, y, z in edges:
        if z == 1:
            working_edges.append((x, y, z))
    
    # Remove working edges that are on the path
    path_set = set()
    for u, v, z in path_nodes:
        path_set.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Find working edges not on the path
    for x, y, z in working_edges:
        if z == 1:
            key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
            if key not in path_set:
                affected_roads.append((x, y, 0))
    
    # Output
    print(len(affected_roads))
    for x, y, z in affected_roads:
        print(x, y, z)

solve()
```

Wait, there's a bug in the BFS - I'm not processing all edges properly, and the condition `if new_d == dist[v]` is not sufficient because we might have multiple ways to reach v.

Actually, we should process all neighbors, and if we find a path to v with the same distance but better profit, we update.

But my algorithm above might miss some paths.

Let me fix the BFS: we should process each node by distance level, and for each node, update all neighbors that are at distance d+1.

Better approach:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
edges = []
total_working = 0

for _ in range(m):
    x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
    graph[x].append((y, z))
    graph[y].append((x, z))
    edges.append((x, y, z))
    if z == 1:
        total_working += 1

# Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
dist[1] = 0
q = deque([1])

while q:
    u = q.popleft()
    for v, z in graph[u]:
        if dist[v] == -1:
            dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
            q.append(v)

# If no path to n
if dist[n] == -1:
    print(0)
else:
    # dp[node][d] = max(working - non_working) for path of length d to node
    # Use dictionary of dictionaries
    dp = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    parent = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(list))  # parent[node][dist] = (prev_node, edge_type)
    
    # Initialize
    dp[1][0] = 0
    
    # BFS by distance levels
    # We'll process level by level
    current_level = 0
    q = deque([1])
    
    # We need to process all nodes by distance
    # Use a queue that stores (node, distance)
    from collections import deque
    q = deque()
    q.append((1, 0))
    
    while q:
        u, d = q.popleft()
        
        # For each neighbor
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            new_d = d + 1
            if new_d > dist[n]:  # skip if too far
                continue
                
            # If we haven't set dist[v] or we can improve profit
            # We need to check if new_d == dist[v]
            if new_d == dist[v]:
                # Calculate new profit
                new_profit = dp[u][d] + (1 if z == 1 else -1)
                if new_profit > dp[v][new_d]:
                    dp[v][new_d] = new_profit
                    parent[v][new_d] = (u, z)
                    q.append((v, new_d))
    
    # Backtrack to find path with maximum profit
    path_edges = []
    cur = n
    cur_d = dist[n]
    
    while cur != 1:
        prev, z = parent[cur][cur_d]
        path_edges.append((cur, prev, z))
        cur = prev
        cur_d -= 1
    
    path_edges.reverse()
    
    # Now form the set of edges on the path (store as (min, max, z))
    path_edge_set = set()
    for u, v, z in path_edges:
        key = (min(u, v), max(u, v), z)
        path_edge_set.add(key)
    
    # Find all working edges that are not on the path
    working_not_on_path = []
    for x, y, z in edges:
        if z == 1:
            key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
            # Check if this edge is on the path (we need to compare with actual z)
            # The edge (x,y) with z=1 is on path if (min(x,y), max(x,y)) is in path_edge_set
            edge_key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
            if edge_key not in path_edge_set:
                working_not_on_path.append((x, y, 0))
    
    # Find edges on path that are not working (z=0) - need to be repaired
    repair_roads = []
    for u, v, z in path_edges:
        if z == 0:
            repair_roads.append((u, v, 1))
    
    # Combine and output
    affected_roads = repair_roads + working_not_on_path
    
    print(len(affected_roads))
    for x, y, z in affected_roads:
        print(x, y, z)
```

But there's a bug: when storing path_edge_set, we store (min, max, z). But when we check the original edges, we also have z. So for an edge (x,y,1), if it's not on the path, we blow it up.

For the path edge set, we need to store the actual (min, max, z) to match.

But in the path_edges, we have (u,v,z), and when we add to set, we have (min(u,v), max(u,v), z). This should work.

Let me test with the examples.

Example 1: n=2, m=1, edge: 1 2 0
- dist[1]=0, dist[2]=1
- dp[1][0]=0
- From 1 to 2: new_d=1, z=0, profit = 0 + (1 if 0==1 else -1) = -1
- dp[2][1] = -1
- Path: (2,1,0) -> reverse to (1,2,0)
- path_edge_set contains (1,2,0)
- working edges: none (since z=0)
- repair_roads: for (1,2,0) - z=0, so add (1,2,1)
- working_not_on_path: none
- output: 1\n1 2 1 ✓

Example 2: n=4, m=4

We want path 1-3-4
