From 1: to 2 (z=1, profit +=1), to 3 (z=0, profit -=1)
From 2: to 3 (z=1, profit +=1), to 4? no
From 3: to 4 (z=1, profit +=1)

We want to reach 4 with dist=2.

Path 1->3->4:
- 1->3: z=0, profit = 0 -1 = -1
- 3->4: z=1, profit = -1 +1 = 0

Path 1->2->3->4? dist[3]=1, so 3 can't be reached in 2 steps from 1? 1->2 (1 step), 2->3 (1 step) so to reach 3 at dist=2? But dist[3]=1, so we can't go to 3 at dist=2.

So only path to 4 is 1->3->4 or 1->2->3->4? 1->2->3->4: dist[2]=1, dist[3]=2? But our BFS says dist[3]=1 because 1->3 directly.

So dist[3] should be 1, so any path to 3 must be in 1 step.

So only paths to 4 of length 2: 1->3->4 (length 2)

So only one path? But the problem says "there are multiple shortest paths" - maybe not in this case.

The example says output is 3, and the roads: 1 2 0, 1 3 1, 2 3 0

So path is 1-3-4? 
- 1->3: z=0, needs to be repaired to z=1
- 3->4: z=1, good
- So path has one non-working edge, so repair cost = 1

Working edges not on path:
- 1->2: z=1, not on path → blow up → cost 1
- 2->3: z=1, not on path → blow up → cost 1
- Total cost = 1 + 2 = 3 ✓

But why is path 1-3-4? Because dist to 4 is 2, and 1->3->4 is length 2.

Is there another path? 1->2->3->4? That's length 3, not shortest.

So only one shortest path: 1->3->4.

In our DP, from 1 to 3 at dist=1: profit = 0 + (-1) = -1
From 1 to 2 at dist=1: profit = 0 + 1 = 1

From 3 to 4 at dist=2: profit = -1 + 1 = 0

No other path to 4 at dist=2.

So path chosen is 1->3->4.

But we have only one path, so it's fine.

Our algorithm should work.

We need to verify that all shortest paths have the same length, and we choose one with minimum cost.

I think the algorithm is correct.

One more thing: in the path reconstruction, when we go from v to u, we store (v,u,z) which we reverse.

In the edge set, we store (min(u,v), max(u,v), z)

In the working_not_on_path, when checking edge (x,y,1), we check if (min(x,y), max(x,y)) is in path_edge_set.

But path_edge_set contains (min, max, z), so we need to match the z as well? 

Yes! When we check if an edge is on the path, we need to know that the edge has the correct z.

For example, suppose we have an edge (1,2,1) that is not on path, but there's a path edge (1,2,0). They are different.

So when we check, we should compare the full (min, max, z).

But in our working_not_on_path, we only have the original edge with z=1.

In path_edge_set, we have (min, max, z) for each edge in the path.

But for an edge in the original graph with z=1, to be on the path, the path must have the same (min, max, z).

So we need to store (min(x,y), max(x,y), z) in path_edge_set.

In the path_edges, we have (u,v,z), so when we create (min(u,v), max(u,v), z), we store it.

So for an original working edge (x,y,1), we check if (min(x,y), max(x,y), 1) is in path_edge_set.

This is correct.

But when we create path_edge_set, we only add the edges in the path with their z.

So it should work.

Let me fix the code.

One issue: we're using `path_edge_set.add(key)` where key = (min(u,v), max(u,v), z). But if multiple edges have same (min, max) with different z, they are different.

But in the graph, between two cities there's at most one road.

So for each pair (x,y), there's at most one edge.

So it's fine.

Final code:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
edges = []

for _ in range(m):
    x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
    graph[x].append((y, z))
    graph[y].append((x, z))
    edges.append((x, y, z))

# Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
dist[1] = 0
q = deque([1])

while q:
    u = q.popleft()
    for v, z in graph[u]:
        if dist[v] == -1:
            dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
            q.append(v)

# If no path to n
if dist[n] == -1:
    print(0)
else:
    # dp[node][d] = max(working - non_working) for path of length d to node
    # Use dictionary of dictionaries
    dp = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    parent = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(list))  # parent[node][dist] = (prev_node, edge_type)
    
    # Initialize
    dp[1][0] = 0
    
    # BFS by distance levels
    q = deque()
    q.append((1, 0))
    
    while q:
        u, d = q.popleft()
        
        # For each neighbor
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            new_d = d + 1
            # Only consider if we can reach v in exactly new_d steps
            if new_d > dist[n] or new_d > dist[v]:  # if already have a shorter path to v, skip
                continue
                
            # If new_d == dist[v], we can update
            if new_d == dist[v]:
                new_profit = dp[u][d] + (1 if z == 1 else -1)
                if new_profit > dp[v][new_d]:
                    dp[v][new_d] = new_profit
                    parent[v][new_d] = (u, z)
                    q.append((v, new_d))
    
    # Backtrack to find path with maximum profit
    path_edges = []
    cur = n
    cur_d = dist[n]
    
    while cur != 1:
        prev, z = parent[cur][cur_d]
        path_edges.append((cur, prev, z))
        cur = prev
        cur_d -= 1
    
    path_edges.reverse()
    
    # Create set of edges on the path with (min, max, z)
    path_edge_set = set()
    for u, v, z in path_edges:
        key = (min(u, v), max(u, v), z)
        path_edge_set.add(key)
    
    # Find roads that need to be blown up (working edges not on path)
    blow_up_roads = []
    for x, y, z in edges:
        if z == 1:  # working edge
            key = (min(x, y), max(x, y), z)
            if key not in path_edge_set:
                blow_up_roads.append((x, y, 0))
    
    # Find roads that need to be repaired (non-working on path)
    repair_roads = []
    for u, v, z in path_edges:
        if z == 0:
            key = (min(u, v), max(u, v), z)
            repair_roads.append((u, v, 1))
    
    # Combine
    affected_roads = repair_roads + blow_up_roads
    
    print(len(affected_roads))
    for x, y, z in affected_roads:
        print(x, y, z)
```

Wait, there's a potential issue: when we check if an edge is on the path, we are only storing the key with z, but the path might have a different z for the same (min, max). But since there's at most one edge between two cities, the (min, max) pair uniquely identifies the edge, and z is part of the edge.

So for each edge, there is exactly one (min, max, z) triple.

So the set comparison is correct.

In the BFS, we have a condition `if new_d > dist[v]` - this is wrong. We should only consider if new_d == dist[v], not >.

Actually, we already have `if new_d == dist[v]` in the condition.

In the code above, I have:
```python
if new_d > dist[n] or new_d > dist[v]:  # if already have a shorter path to v, skip
```
But this is incorrect - if new_d > dist[v], it means dist[v] is smaller, so we can't reach v in new_d steps if dist[v] is smaller. So we skip.

But in the BFS, we only process when new_d == dist[v], so we don't need to check this in the inner loop.

Actually, we already have the condition `if new_d == dist[v]` before the inner loop. So we can remove that outer condition.

Let me fix:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
edges = []

for _ in range(m):
    x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
    graph[x].append((y, z))
    graph[y].append((x, z))
    edges.append((x, y, z))

# Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
dist[1] = 0
q = deque([1])

while q:
    u = q.popleft()
    for v, z in graph[u]:
        if dist[v] == -1:
            dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
            q.append(v)

# If no path to n
if dist[n] == -1:
    print(0)
else:
    # dp[node][d] = max(working - non_working) for path of length d to node
    dp = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    parent = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(list))  # parent[node][dist] = (prev_node, edge_type)
    
    # Initialize
    dp[1][0] = 0
    
    # BFS by distance levels
    q = deque()
    q.append((1, 0))
    
    while q:
        u, d = q.popleft()
        
        # For each neighbor
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            new_d = d + 1
            # Only consider if we can reach v in exactly new_d steps (which equals dist[v])
            if new_d > dist[n]:
                continue
            if new_d > dist[v]:  # already have a shorter path to v, can't improve
                continue
            # new_d == dist[v] by the above condition
            new_profit = dp[u][d] + (1 if z == 1 else -1)
            if new_profit > dp[v][new_d]:
                dp[v][new_d] = new_profit
                parent[v][new_d] = (u, z)
                q.append((v, new_d))
    
    # Backtrack to find path with maximum profit
    path_edges = []
    cur = n
    cur_d = dist[n]
    
    while cur != 1:
        prev, z = parent[cur][cur_d]
        path_edges.append((cur, prev, z))
        cur = prev
        cur_d -= 1
    
    path_edges.reverse()
    
    # Create set of edges on the path with (min, max, z)
    path_edge_set = set()
    for u, v, z in path_edges:
        key = (min(u, v), max(u, v), z)
        path_edge_set.add(key)
    
    # Find roads that need to be blown up (working edges not on path)
    blow_up_roads = []
    for x, y, z in edges:
        if z == 1:  # working edge
            key = (min(x, y), max(x, y), z)
            if key not in path_edge_set:
                blow_up_roads.append((x, y, 0))
    
    # Find roads that need to be repaired (non-working on path)
    repair_roads = []
    for u, v, z in path_edges:
        if z == 0:
            key = (min(u, v), max(u, v), z)
            repair_roads.append((u, v, 1))
    
    # Combine
    affected_roads = repair_roads + blow_up_roads
    
    print(len(affected_roads))
    for x, y, z in affected_roads:
        print(x, y, z)
```

This should be correct.

Let me test with example 1:
- n=2, m=1: edge (1,2,0)
- dist[1]=0, dist[2]=1
- q: (1,0)
- u=1, d=0, v=2, z=0, new_d=1
- new_d=1 <= dist[2]=1 and new_d <= dist[2], and new_d == dist[2] (1==1)
- new_profit = dp[1][0] + (1 if 0==1 else -1) = 0 + (-1) = -1
- dp[2][1] = -1, parent[2][1] = (1,0)
- Backtrack: cur=2, cur_d=1, prev=1, z=0, path_edges = [(2,1,0)], reverse to [(1,2,0)]
- path_edge_set = {(1,2,0)}
- blow_up_roads: no working edges, so empty
- repair_roads: for (1,2,0) with z=0, so add (1,2,1)
- output: 1\n1 2 1 ✓

Example 2:
- Edges: (1,2,1), (1,3,0), (2,3,1), (3,4,1)
- dist: 1:0, 2:1, 3:1, 4:2
- From 1: to 2 (z=1, profit=1), to 3 (z=0, profit=-1)
- From 2: to 3 (z=1, profit=1+1=2), but to 3 at new_d=2? dist[3]=1, so new_d=2 > dist[3]=1, skip
- From 3: to 4 (z=1, profit for 3 at d=1: if came from 1, profit=-1 +1=0; if came from 2, profit=1+1=2, then +1=3)
- From 3, d=1: if we have path to 3 with profit=1 (from 2) then to 4: profit=1+1=2
- From 3, d=1: if we have path with profit=-1 (from 1) then to 4: profit=-1+1=0
- So best profit at 4 is 2? 
- But in the given output, they have path 1-3-4, which has only one working edge (3-4), and one non-working (1-3) so cost = 1 (repair) + 2 (blow up 1-2 and 2-3) = 3
- But our DP would find path 1-2-3-4? Can we reach 3 in 2 steps? dist[3]=1, so no.
- Actually, from 1 to 2 (1 step), 2 to 3 (1 step) → to reach 3 at dist=2, but dist[3]=1, so it's not possible.
- So only way to reach 4 is 1→3→4 at dist=2
- At 3: we have two ways to reach 3 at dist=1: from 1 (profit=-1) and from 2 (profit=1)
- Then from 3 to 4: profit = -1 + 1 = 0 or 1 + 1 = 2
- So we pick the one with profit 2, which is path 1→2→3→4? No, that would require reaching 3 at dist=2, which is not allowed.
- Actually, from 1 to 2 at dist=1 (profit=1), then from 2 to 3 at dist=2? But dist[3]=1, so dist[3] is 1, so we can't reach 3 at dist=2.
- So only possibility is from 3 at dist=1 to 4 at dist=2, with profit: from 1→3 (profit=-1) then 3→4 (profit=-1+1=0)
- So only profit 0 at 4.
- But that gives a path with cost = 1 (repair for 1-3) + 2 (blow up 1-2 and 2-3) = 3
- And that matches the expected output.
- So our algorithm will choose the path with maximum profit, which is 0 (since no better path exists).
- So it will choose path 1→3→4 or possibly 1→2→3? But 3 at dist=2 is impossible.
- Actually, in the BFS, when we process node 1 at d=0, we go to neighbors:
  - 2: new_d=1, which equals dist[2]=1, so add (1,2) with profit=1
  - 3: new_d=1, which equals dist[3]=1, so add (1,3) with profit=-1
- Then when processing node 2 at d=1: go to 3 (v=3, new_d=2), but dist[3]=1, so new_d=2 > dist[3]=1, so skip
- Then processing node 3 at d=1: go to 4 (new_d=2), dist[4]=2, so new_d=2==dist[4], so update: from 3 with profit=-1, new_profit = -1 + 1 = 0, so dp[4][2]=0
- So path is 1→3→4 with profit 0
- Then we output repair for 1-3 (z=0), and blow up 1-2 and 2-3 (both z=1)
- So output: 1 2 0, 1 3 1, 2 3 0 → which matches expected output!

So it works.

Final code:

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
edges = []

for _ in range(m):
    x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
    graph[x].append((y, z))
    graph[y].append((x, z))
    edges.append((x, y, z))

# Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
dist[1] = 0
q = deque([1])

while q:
    u = q.popleft()
    for v, z in graph[u]:
        if dist[v] == -1:
            dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
            q.append(v)

# If no path to n
if dist[n] == -1:
    print(0)
else:
    # dp[node][d] = max(working - non_working) for path of length d to node
    dp = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(int))
    parent = defaultdict(lambda: defaultdict(list))  # parent[node][dist] = (prev_node, edge_type)
    
    # Initialize
    dp[1][0] = 0
    
    # BFS by distance levels
    q = deque()
    q.append((1, 0))
    
    while q:
        u, d = q.popleft()
        
        # For each neighbor
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            new_d = d + 1
            # Only consider if we can reach v in exactly new_d steps (which equals dist[v])
            if new_d > dist[n]:
                continue
            if new_d > dist[v]:  # already have a shorter path to v, can't improve
                continue
            # new_d == dist[v] by the above condition
            new_profit = dp[u][d] + (1 if z == 1 else -1)
            if new_profit > dp[v][new_d]:
                dp[v][new_d] = new_profit
                parent[v][new_d] = (u, z)
                q.append((v, new_d))
    
    # Backtrack to find path with maximum profit
    path_edges = []
    cur = n
    cur_d = dist[n]
    
    while cur != 1:
        prev, z = parent[cur][cur_d]
        path_edges.append((cur, prev, z))
        cur = prev
        cur_d -= 1
    
    path_edges.reverse()
    
    # Create set of edges on the path with (min, max, z)
    path_edge_set = set()
    for u, v, z in path_edges:
        key = (min(u, v), max(u, v), z)
        path_edge_set.add(key)
    
    # Find roads that need to be blown up (working edges not on path)
    blow_up_roads = []
    for x, y, z in edges:
        if z == 1:  # working edge
            key = (min(x, y), max(x, y), z)
            if key not in path_edge_set:
                blow_up_roads.append((x, y, 0))
    
    # Find roads that need to be repaired (non-working on path)
    repair_roads = []
    for u, v, z in path_edges:
        if z == 0:
            key = (min(u, v), max(u, v), z)
            repair_roads.append((u, v, 1))
    
    # Combine
    affected_roads = repair_roads + blow_up_roads
    
    print(len(affected_roads))
    for x, y, z in affected_roads:
        print(x, y, z)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Breaking Good is a new video game which a lot of gamers want to have. There is a certain level in the game that is really difficult even for experienced gamers.

Walter William, the main character of the game, wants to join a gang called Los Hermanos (The Brothers). The gang controls the whole country which consists of n cities with m bidirectional roads connecting them. There is no road is connecting a city to itself and for any two cities there is at most one road between them. The country is connected, in the other words, it is possible to reach any city from any other city using the given roads. 

The roads aren't all working. There are some roads which need some more work to be performed to be completely functioning.

The gang is going to rob a bank! The bank is located in city 1. As usual, the hardest part is to escape to their headquarters where the police can't get them. The gang's headquarters is in city n. To gain the gang's trust, Walter is in charge of this operation, so he came up with a smart plan.

First of all the path which they are going to use on their way back from city 1 to their headquarters n must be as short as possible, since it is important to finish operation as fast as possible.

Then, gang has to blow up all other roads in country that don't lay on this path, in order to prevent any police reinforcements. In case of non-working road, they don't have to blow up it as it is already malfunctional. 

If the chosen path has some roads that doesn't work they'll have to repair those roads before the operation.

Walter discovered that there was a lot of paths that satisfied the condition of being shortest possible so he decided to choose among them a path that minimizes the total number of affected roads (both roads that have to be blown up and roads to be repaired).

Can you help Walter complete his task and gain the gang's trust?

The first line of input contains two integers n, m (2 ≤ n ≤ 10^5, $0 \leq m \leq \operatorname{min}(\frac{n(n - 1)}{2}, 10^{5})$), the number of cities and number of roads respectively.

-----Output-----

In the first line output one integer k, the minimum possible number of roads affected by gang.

In the following k lines output three integers describing roads that should be affected. Each line should contain three integers x, y, z (1 ≤ x, y ≤ n, $z \in \{0,1 \}$), cities connected by a road and the new state of a road. z = 1 indicates that the road between cities x and y should be repaired and z = 0 means that road should be blown up. 

You may output roads in any order. Each affected road should appear exactly once. You may output cities connected by a single road in any order. If you output a road, it's original state should be different from z.

After performing all operations accroding to your plan, there should remain working only roads lying on some certain shortest past between city 1 and n.

If there are multiple optimal answers output any.

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
        graph[x].append((y, z))
        graph[y].append((x, z))
        edges.append((x, y, z))
    
    # If only one city, but n >= 2 by constraint
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Step 1: Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    dist[1] = 0
    queue = deque([1])
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            if dist[v] == -1:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # If no path from 1 to n (shouldn't happen as graph is connected)
    if dist[n] == -1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Step 2: Use DP to find the path from 1 to n with maximum working edges
    # max_working[i] = maximum number of working edges on a shortest path from 1 to i
    max_working = [0] * (n + 1)
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    edge_state = [0] * (n + 1)  # edge_state[i] will store the z value of the edge from parent[i] to i
    
    # Process nodes in order of increasing distance
    # First, get all nodes in order of distance
    nodes_by_dist = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if dist[i] != -1:
            nodes_by_dist[dist[i]].append(i)
    
    # Process in increasing order of distance
    for d in range(n + 1):
        for node in nodes_by_dist[d]:
            if node == 1:
                continue  # already handled
                
            for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
                if dist[neighbor] == dist[node] + 1:
                    # This edge can be part of a shortest path from 1 to neighbor
                    new_working = max_working[node]
                    if z == 1:
                        new_working += 1
                    
                    if new_working > max_working[neighbor]:
                        max_working[neighbor] = new_working
                        parent[neighbor] = node
                        edge_state[neighbor] = z
    
    # Reconstruct path from n to 1
    path = []
    current = n
    while current != 1:
        path.append(current)
        current = parent[current]
    path.append(1)
    path.reverse()
    
    # Now, generate the operations
    operations = []
    
    # Step 3: For each edge on the path, determine if it needs repair
    # First, collect all edges on the path
    path_edges = set()
    for i in range(len(path) - 1):
        u, v = path[i], path[i + 1]
        # Find the edge between u and v in the original graph
        # We need to know the state of this edge
        for neighbor, z in graph[u]:
            if neighbor == v:
                path_edges.add((u, v, z))
                break
        # Also add (v, u, z) - but we'll use the same z
        for neighbor, z in graph[v]:
            if neighbor == u:
                path_edges.add((v, u, z))
                break
    
    # Actually, since we stored edge_state for each node, we can use that
    # But let's rebuild the edge states for the path
    path_edge_states = {}
    for i in range(len(path) - 1):
        u, v = path[i], path[i + 1]
        # Find the state of edge (u,v)
        found = False
        for neighbor, z in graph[u]:
            if neighbor == v:
                path_edge_states[(min(u,v), max(u,v))] = z
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            for neighbor, z in graph[v]:
                if neighbor == u:
                    path_edge_states[(min(u,v), max(u,v))] = z
                    found = True
                    break
    
    # Now, for each edge in the entire graph:
    # - If it's on the path and it's broken (z=0), we need to repair it (z=1)
    # - If it's not on the path and it's working (z=1), we need to blow it up (z=0)
    
    # First, create a set of edges that are on the path
    path_edge_set = set()
    for i in range(len(path) - 1):
        u, v = path[i], path[i + 1]
        path_edge_set.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Collect all edges in the input (in canonical form)
    all_edges = set()
    for x, y, z in edges:
        all_edges.add((min(x, y), max(x, y)))
    
    # Find affected roads
    affected = []
    
    # Case 1: roads on path that are broken (need repair)
    for (u, v), z in path_edge_states.items():
        if z == 0:  # broken, needs repair
            affected.append((u, v, 1))
    
    # Case 2: roads not on path that are working (need to be blown up)
    for (u, v), z in all_edges:
        if (u, v) not in path_edge_set and z == 1:
            affected.append((u, v, 0))
    
    # Output
    print(len(affected))
    for op in affected:
        print(op[0], op[1], op[2])

solve()
```

Wait, I see a problem. In the DP step, when I process a node, I'm overwriting the max_working value. But I need to consider all paths, and there might be multiple ways to reach a node with the same distance but different working edge counts.

The algorithm above should work, but I need to be careful about the edge representation.

Let me trace through the examples.

Possible paths of length 3:
- 1-2-3-8
- 1-4-6-8
- 1-5-7-8
- 1-2-3-8? (1-2:0, 2-3:1, 3-8:0) → working edges: only 2-3 → 1 working edge
- 1-4-6-8: (1,4):1, (4,6):1, (6,8):0 → working edges: 1,4 (two edges) → 2 working edges
- 1-5-7-8: (1,5):1, (5,7):0, (7,8):? → 7 is connected to 8? 8 is connected to 7? Yes, edge 8-7:0 → so (7,8):0 → working edges: only (1,5) → 1 working edge

So path 1-4-6-8 has 2 working edges, which is maximum.

In the expected output: 3 operations: 2 3 0, 1 5 0, 6 8 1

Let me check what this means:
- 2 3 0: edge (2,3) was working (z=1), now becomes 0 → blown up
- 1 5 0: edge (1,5) was working (z=1), now becomes 0 → blown up  
- 6 8 1: edge (6,8) was broken (z=0), now becomes 1 → repaired

So for path 1-4-6-8:
- On path: edges (1,4), (4,6), (6,8)
- (1,4): z=1 (working) - no repair needed
- (4,6): z=1 (working) - no repair needed  
- (6,8): z=0 (broken) - needs repair → this is one operation
- Not on path and working: 
  - (1,2): z=0 → already broken, no action
  - (1,5): z=1 → working, not on path → needs to be blown up → one operation
  - (2,3): z=1 → working, not on path → needs to be blown up → one operation
  - (8,3): z=0 → broken, no action
  - (8,7): z=0 → broken, no action
  - (5,7): z=0 → broken, no action
- Total operations: 1 (repair 6-8) + 1 (blow up 1-5) + 1 (blow up 2-3) = 3 ✓

But in my algorithm, I would get:
- Path 1-4-6-8
- On path: (1,4): working (z=1) → no repair, (4,6): working (z=1) → no repair, (6,8): broken (z=0) → needs repair → 1 repair
- Not on path and working: 
  - (1,2): z=0 → no
  - (1,5): z=1 → working, not on path → needs blow up
  - (2,3): z=1 → working, not on path → needs blow up
  - (5,7): z=0 → no
  - (8,7): z=0 → no
- So operations: (6,8,1), (1,5,0), (2,3,0)

But the expected output is: 2 3 0, 1 5 0, 6 8 1

Same operations, just different order - so it's valid.

In my algorithm, I'm not storing which specific edges are not on the path with z=1, I'm just finding all such edges.

So the code should be correct.

One issue: I need to make sure I process nodes in order of distance and that when I find a path to a node, it's a shortest path.

Also, in the edge state lookup, I need to be careful.

Let me fix the code:

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
        graph[x].append((y, z))
        graph[y].append((x, z))
        edges.append((x, y, z))
    
    # If only one city, but n >= 2 by constraint
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Step 1: Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    dist[1] = 0
    queue = deque([1])
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            if dist[v] == -1:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # If no path from 1 to n (shouldn't happen as graph is connected)
    if dist[n] == -1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Step 2: Use DP to find the path from 1 to n with maximum working edges
    max_working = [0] * (n + 1)
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    # edge_state[i] = z value of the edge from parent[i] to i (for i > 1)
    edge_state = [0] * (n + 1)  # will store the z value for the edge from parent to current
    
    # Process nodes in order of increasing distance
    # First, get all nodes in order of distance
    nodes_by_dist = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if dist[i] != -1:
            nodes_by_dist[dist[i]].append(i)
    
    # Process in increasing order of distance
    for d in range(n + 1):
        for node in nodes_by_dist[d]:
            if node == 1:
                continue  # already handled
                
            for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
                if dist[neighbor] == dist[node] + 1:
                    # This edge can be part of a shortest path from 1 to neighbor
                    new_working = max_working[node]
                    if z == 1:
                        new_working += 1
                    
                    if new_working > max_working[neighbor]:
                        max_working[neighbor] = new_working
                        parent[neighbor] = node
                        edge_state[neighbor] = z
    
    # Reconstruct path from n to 1
    path = []
    current = n
    while current != 1:
        path.append(current)
        current = parent[current]
    path.append(1)
    path.reverse()
    
    # Now, generate the operations
    operations = []
    
    # Step 3: For each edge on the path, determine if it needs repair
    # First, collect all edges on the path in canonical form
    path_edges = set()
    for i in range(len(path) - 1):
        u, v = path[i], path[i + 1]
        path_edges.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Find all edges in the input with canonical form
    # We need to know the state of each edge
    edge_map = {}
    for x, y, z in edges:
        key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
        edge_map[key] = z
    
    # Case 1: roads on path that are broken (need repair)
    for (u, v) in path_edges:
        z = edge_map[(u, v)]
        if z == 0:  # broken, needs repair
            operations.append((u, v, 1))
    
    # Case 2: roads not on path that are working (need to be blown up)
    for (u, v), z in edge_map.items():
        if (u, v) not in path_edges and z == 1:
            operations.append((u, v, 0))
    
    # Output
    print(len(operations))
    for op in operations:
        print(op[0], op[1], op[2])

solve()
```

This should work. Let me check if it handles bidirectional edges correctly.

For edge (u,v), we store it as (min(u,v), max(u,v)) in both edge_map and path_edges, so it's canonical.

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
        graph[x].append((y, z))
        graph[y].append((x, z))
        edges.append((x, y, z))
    
    # If only one city, but n >= 2 by constraint
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Step 1: Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    dist[1] = 0
    queue = deque([1])
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            if dist[v] == -1:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # If no path from 1 to n (shouldn't happen as graph is connected)
    if dist[n] == -1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Step 2: Use DP to find the path from 1 to n with maximum working edges
    max_working = [0] * (n + 1)
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    # edge_state[i] = z value of the edge from parent[i] to i (for i > 1)
    edge_state = [0] * (n + 1)  # will store the z value for the edge from parent to current
    
    # Process nodes in order of increasing distance
    # First, get all nodes in order of distance
    nodes_by_dist = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if dist[i] != -1:
            nodes_by_dist[dist[i]].append(i)
    
    # Process in increasing order of distance
    for d in range(n + 1):
        for node in nodes_by_dist[d]:
            if node == 1:
                continue  # already handled
                
            for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
                if dist[neighbor] == dist[node] + 1:
                    # This edge can be part of a shortest path from 1 to neighbor
                    new_working = max_working[node]
                    if z == 1:
                        new_working += 1
                    
                    if new_working > max_working[neighbor]:
                        max_working[neighbor] = new_working
                        parent[neighbor] = node
                        edge_state[neighbor] = z
    
    # Reconstruct path from n to 1
    path = []
    current = n
    while current != 1:
        path.append(current)
        current = parent[current]
    path.append(1)
    path.reverse()
    
    # Now, generate the operations
    operations = []
    
    # Step 3: For each edge on the path, determine if it needs repair
    # First, collect all edges on the path in canonical form
    path_edges = set()
    for i in range(len(path) - 1):
        u, v = path[i], path[i + 1]
        path_edges.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Find all edges in the input with canonical form
    # We need to know the state of each edge
    edge_map = {}
    for x, y, z in edges:
        key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
        edge_map[key] = z
    
    # Case 1: roads on path that are broken (need repair)
    for (u, v) in path_edges:
        z = edge_map[(u, v)]
        if z == 0:  # broken, needs repair
            operations.append((u, v, 1))
    
    # Case 2: roads not on path that are working (need to be blown up)
    for (u, v), z in edge_map.items():
        if (u, v) not in path_edges and z == 1:
            operations.append((u, v, 0))
    
    # Output
    print(len(operations))
    for op in operations:
        print(op[0], op[1], op[2])

solve()
```

from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y, z = map(int, input().split())
        graph[x].append((y, z))
        graph[y].append((x, z))
        edges.append((x, y, z))
    
    # If only one city, but n >= 2 by constraint
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Step 1: Find shortest distance from 1 to n using BFS
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    dist[1] = 0
    queue = deque([1])
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v, z in graph[u]:
            if dist[v] == -1:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # If no path from 1 to n (shouldn't happen as graph is connected)
    if dist[n] == -1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Step 2: Use DP to find the path from 1 to n with maximum working edges
    max_working = [0] * (n + 1)
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    # edge_state[i] = z value of the edge from parent[i] to i (for i > 1)
    edge_state = [0] * (n + 1)  # will store the z value for the edge from parent to current
    
    # Process nodes in order of increasing distance
    # First, get all nodes in order of distance
    nodes_by_dist = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if dist[i] != -1:
            nodes_by_dist[dist[i]].append(i)
    
    # Process in increasing order of distance
    for d in range(n + 1):
        for node in nodes_by_dist[d]:
            if node == 1:
                continue  # already handled
                
            for neighbor, z in graph[node]:
                if dist[neighbor] == dist[node] + 1:
                    # This edge can be part of a shortest path from 1 to neighbor
                    new_working = max_working[node]
                    if z == 1:
                        new_working += 1
                    
                    if new_working > max_working[neighbor]:
                        max_working[neighbor] = new_working
                        parent[neighbor] = node
                        edge_state[neighbor] = z
    
    # Reconstruct path from n to 1
    path = []
    current = n
    while current != 1:
        path.append(current)
        current = parent[current]
    path.append(1)
    path.reverse()
    
    # Now, generate the operations
    operations = []
    
    # Step 3: For each edge on the path, determine if it needs repair
    # First, collect all edges on the path in canonical form
    path_edges = set()
    for i in range(len(path) - 1):
        u, v = path[i], path[i + 1]
        path_edges.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Find all edges in the input with canonical form
    # We need to know the state of each edge
    edge_map = {}
    for x, y, z in edges:
        key = (min(x, y), max(x, y))
        edge_map[key] = z
    
    # Case 1: roads on path that are broken (need repair)
    for (u, v) in path_edges:
        z = edge_map[(u, v)]
        if z == 0:  # broken, needs repair
            operations.append((u, v, 1))
    
    # Case 2: roads not on path that are working (need to be blown up)
    for (u, v), z in edge_map.items():
        if (u, v) not in path_edges and z == 1:
            operations.append((u, v, 0))
    
    # Output
    print(len(operations))
    for op in operations:
        print(op[0], op[1], op[2])

solve()

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Little Petya loves playing with squares. Mum bought him a square 2n × 2n in size. Petya marked a cell inside the square and now he is solving the following task.

The task is to draw a broken line that would go along the grid lines and that would cut the square into two equal parts. The cutting line should not have any common points with the marked cell and the resulting two parts should be equal up to rotation.

Petya wants to determine whether it is possible to cut the square in the required manner given the sizes of the square side and the coordinates of the marked cell. Help him.

Input

The first line contains three space-separated integers 2n, x and y (2 ≤ 2n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ x, y ≤ 2n), representing the length of a square's side and the coordinates of the marked cell. It is guaranteed that 2n is even.

The coordinates of the marked cell are represented by a pair of numbers x y, where x represents the number of the row and y represents the number of the column. The rows and columns are numbered by consecutive integers from 1 to 2n. The rows are numbered from top to bottom and the columns are numbered from the left to the right.

Output

If the square is possible to cut, print "YES", otherwise print "NO" (without the quotes).

Examples

Input

4 1 1


Output

YES


Input

2 2 2


Output

NO

- A horizontal cut at row 3 would go through (3,1), (3,2), (3,3), (3,4) - this touches (3,3).
- A diagonal cut from top-left to bottom-right would go through (1,1), (2,2), (3,3), (4,4) - touches (3,3).
- A diagonal cut from top-right to bottom-left would go through (1,4), (2,3), (3,2), (4,1) - this touches (2,3), but not (3,3).

The cut must go along grid lines, so it can only move horizontally or vertically between cell intersections.

A cell at (x,y) is touched if the cut passes through one of its edges or vertices.

The cut at y=2.5 from x=1 to x=4 passes through the middle of row 2 and 3, so it touches the edges between row 2 and 3 in every column. Does it touch cell (3,3)? Cell (3,3) is row 3, column 3. The cut at y=2.5 is below row 3, so it doesn't touch the top edge of cell (3,3). It touches the middle of the vertical line at x=3 at y=2.5, which is on the edge between row 2 and 3, column 3. 

In a grid, cells are defined by their boundaries. Cell (i,j) occupies the area from row i-1 to i, column j-1 to j.

The edge at y=2.5 in column 3 is the edge between row 2 and 3 of cell (3,3). So if the cut passes through this edge, it would be touching cell (3,3).

Similarly, any cut that goes through the interior of any cell boundary would be touching that cell.

For a cell at (x,y), it is touched by a cut if the cut passes through any of its four edges.

For the horizontal cut at y=2.5 from x=1 to x=4, it touches the edge between row 2 and 3 in every column. In particular, in column 3, it touches the edge between row 2 and 3 of cell (3,3), so it touches cell (3,3).

Similarly, any horizontal or vertical cut through the center will pass through some cell's edges.

But can we make a broken line that avoids cell (3,3)?

The cut from (1,1) to (1,4) is along the top edge, which doesn't touch any cells' interiors. Then from (1,4) to (4,4) along the right edge - this passes through the right edge of cells (1,4), (2,4), (3,4), (4,4). The right edge of cell (3,4) is at x=4, so it touches cell (3,4). But not (3,3).

The cell (3,3) is in the middle. Does the cut pass through it? The only possible points are on the edges. For example, does it pass through the top edge of (3,3)? To touch cell (3,3), the cut would need to pass through one of its four edges: top (y=3), bottom (y=4), left (x=3), right (x=4).

- It doesn't pass through y=3 in any segment
- It doesn't pass through y=4 in any segment except at the corners
- It doesn't pass through x=3 in any segment (it only has segments at x=1 and x=4)

So this broken line avoids cell (3,3).

Therefore, I'll go with this solution.

```python
# Read input
line = input().split()
size = int(line[0])
x = int(line[1])
y = int(line[2])

# Calculate n
n = size // 2

# Check if the marked cell is at (n+1, n+1)
if x == n + 1 and y == n + 1:
    print("NO")
else:
    print("YES")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Little Petya loves playing with squares. Mum bought him a square 2n × 2n in size. Petya marked a cell inside the square and now he is solving the following task.

The task is to draw a broken line that would go along the grid lines and that would cut the square into two equal parts. The cutting line should not have any common points with the marked cell and the resulting two parts should be equal up to rotation.

Petya wants to determine whether it is possible to cut the square in the required manner given the sizes of the square side and the coordinates of the marked cell. Help him.

Input

The first line contains three space-separated integers 2n, x and y (2 ≤ 2n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ x, y ≤ 2n), representing the length of a square's side and the coordinates of the marked cell. It is guaranteed that 2n is even.

The coordinates of the marked cell are represented by a pair of numbers x y, where x represents the number of the row and y represents the number of the column. The rows and columns are numbered by consecutive integers from 1 to 2n. The rows are numbered from top to bottom and the columns are numbered from the left to the right.

Output

If the square is possible to cut, print "YES", otherwise print "NO" (without the quotes).

Examples

Input

4 1 1


Output

YES


Input

2 2 2


Output

NO

y=3, y < 2? False, y > 3? 3>3? False → y part false
So overall false → NO

Check: does the cut touch cell (1,3)?

So yes, it touches the boundary of cell (1,3) → should be NO → correct.

Case: 4×4, n=2, cell (3,1)
x=3, y=1

y < 2? 1<2 → True → y part true

y=1: horizontal line y=2.5, y in [2,3]? 1 not in [2,3] → doesn't touch

x=1, y=2

y=2, y < 2? False, y > 3? False → y part false

Case: 4×4, n=2, cell (1,4)
x=1, y=4
x < 2 → True
y > 3? 4>3 → True → both true → YES

Check: does the cut touch cell (1,4)?
Vertical line x=2.5: touches cells with x in [2,3] → x=1 is not in [2,3] → doesn't touch
Horizontal line y=2.5: touches cells with y in [2,3] → y=4 not in [2,3] → doesn't touch
So no common points → YES → correct.

Therefore, the solution is correct.

```python
side, x, y = map(int, input().split())
n = side // 2

# Check if the marked cell is such that the cut path doesn't touch its boundary
# The cut path touches cell (x,y) if either:
# - n <= x <= n+1 (vertical line touches)
# - n <= y <= n+1 (horizontal line touches)
# So we avoid if neither condition holds
if (x < n or x > n + 1) and (y < n or y > n + 1):
    print("YES")
else:
    print("NO")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Little Petya loves playing with squares. Mum bought him a square 2n × 2n in size. Petya marked a cell inside the square and now he is solving the following task.

The task is to draw a broken line that would go along the grid lines and that would cut the square into two equal parts. The cutting line should not have any common points with the marked cell and the resulting two parts should be equal up to rotation.

Petya wants to determine whether it is possible to cut the square in the required manner given the sizes of the square side and the coordinates of the marked cell. Help him.

Input

The first line contains three space-separated integers 2n, x and y (2 ≤ 2n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ x, y ≤ 2n), representing the length of a square's side and the coordinates of the marked cell. It is guaranteed that 2n is even.

The coordinates of the marked cell are represented by a pair of numbers x y, where x represents the number of the row and y represents the number of the column. The rows and columns are numbered by consecutive integers from 1 to 2n. The rows are numbered from top to bottom and the columns are numbered from the left to the right.

Output

If the square is possible to cut, print "YES", otherwise print "NO" (without the quotes).

Examples

Input

4 1 1


Output

YES


Input

2 2 2


Output

NO

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 2n × 2n square grid

3. The cutting line must not touch the marked cell
4. The two resulting parts must be equal up to rotation (meaning they have the same shape, just rotated)
5. We need to determine if such a cut is possible

## Key Insights

Since we need to cut the square into two equal parts that are equal up to rotation, and the square is symmetric, we need to think about what kind of cuts would work.

The most natural cuts would be:
- Horizontal cuts (dividing into two equal rows)
- Vertical cuts (dividing into two equal columns)
- Diagonal cuts (dividing along diagonals)

But since we need the resulting parts to be equal up to rotation, let's consider what symmetry properties are needed.

For a cut to divide a square into two equal parts that are rotationally equivalent, the cut must pass through the center of the square. This is because rotation symmetry around the center would map one part to the other.

So any valid cut must pass through the center point of the square.

The center of a 2n × 2n square with coordinates from (1,1) to (2n,2n) is at position ((2n+1)/2, (2n+1)/2).

Since 2n is even, the center is at (n + 0.5, n + 0.5).

For a broken line to pass through the center and cut the square into two equal parts, it needs to go from one side to the opposite side, passing through the center.

The key constraint is that the cut cannot touch the marked cell.

Similarly, for a cell (x,y), the center point (n+0.5, n+0.5) will be in cell (x,y) if and only if:
- n+0.5 is in [x-1, x] and in [y-1, y]

That is:
- x-1 ≤ n+0.5 ≤ x
- y-1 ≤ n+0.5 ≤ y

From x-1 ≤ n+0.5 ≤ x, we get:
x-1 ≤ n+0.5 and n+0.5 ≤ x
So x ≥ n+0.5 and x ≥ n+0.5, and x ≤ n+0.5 and x ≥ n+0.5

From x-1 ≤ n+0.5: x ≤ n+1.5
From n+0.5 ≤ x: x ≥ n+0.5

Similarly for y: y ≥ n+0.5 and y ≤ n+1.5

Since x is integer, x ≥ n+0.5 means x ≥ n+1 (since x is integer)
x ≤ n+1.5 means x ≤ n+1

So x = n+1

Similarly, y = n+1

Therefore, the center point (n+0.5, n+0.5) is in the cell (x,y) only if x = n+1 and y = n+1.

In other words, only the cell at the center of the grid (specifically the cell at (n+1, n+1)) contains the center point.

For any other cell, the center point is not in that cell.

Therefore, if the marked cell is not (n+1, n+1), then the center point is not in that cell, so we can avoid it by choosing a cut that doesn't go through that cell.

For example, in a 4×4 grid, n=2, center at (2.5,2.5), which is in cell (3,3) because:
- x from 2 to 3, so 2.5 is in [2,3]
- y from 2 to 3, so 2.5 is in [2,3]

So only cell (3,3) contains the center point.

For example, if we have a marked cell at (1,1) in 4×4, then the center point (2.5,2.5) is not in cell (1,1) (which has x from 0 to 1, y from 0 to 1), so we can choose a cut that avoids cell (1,1).

For example, a vertical cut at x=2.5 would go through x=2.5, which has y from 0 to 4.

Cell (1,1) has x from 0 to 1, so x=2.5 is not in [0,1], so the cut doesn't pass through cell (1,1).

Similarly, it won't pass through other cells with x<2.5.

So the only cell that contains the center point is (n+1, n+1).

Therefore, we can only avoid the marked cell if the marked cell is not (n+1, n+1).

Wait, in the 2×2 example: n=1, center at (1.5,1.5), which is in cell (2,2) because x from 1 to 2, y from 1 to 2.

So cell (2,2) contains the center.

For marked cell (2,2), it is cell (2,2), so it contains the center, so any cut through center will pass through it, so answer is NO.

For marked cell (1,1) in 2×2: cell (1,1) has x from 0 to 1, y from 0 to 1. Center (1.5,1.5) is not in this cell, so we can avoid it by choosing a cut that doesn't go through it.

For example, a vertical cut at x=1.5 would go through x=1.5, which is in cells with x from 1 to 2, so it goes through cells (2,1) and (2,2), not (1,1).

Similarly, a horizontal cut at y=1.5 would go through cells (1,2) and (2,2), not (1,1).

So (1,1) should be avoidable.

But the example input is "2 2 2" which gives "NO", and "4 1 1" gives "YES".

In "4 1 1": 2n=4, so n=2, center at (2.5,2.5), which is in cell (3,3). Marked cell is (1,1), which is not (3,3), so should be avoidable -> YES.

In "2 2 2": 2n=2, n=1, center at (1.5,1.5), in cell (2,2). Marked cell is (2,2), which is (n+1, n+1), so impossible -> NO.

Therefore, the condition is: if the marked cell is at (n+1, n+1), then answer is "NO", otherwise "YES".

Let me verify with the examples.

Example 1: 4 1 1
2n=4, so n=2, n+1=3. Marked cell is (1,1), not (3,3) -> YES. ✓

Example 2: 2 2 2
2n=2, n=1, n+1=2. Marked cell is (2,2), which is (2,2) -> NO. ✓

Perfect.

So the solution is:
- Read 2n, x, y
- n = 2n // 2
- If x == n+1 and y == n+1, then print "NO", else print "YES"

```python
side, x, y = map(int, input().split())
n = side // 2
if x == n + 1 and y == n + 1:

else:
    print("YES")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 2n × 2n square grid
2. We need to draw a broken line along grid lines that cuts the square into two equal parts
3. The cutting line must not touch the marked cell
4. The two resulting parts must be equal up to rotation (meaning they have the same shape, just rotated)
5. We need to determine if such a cut is possible

## Key Insights

Since we need to cut the square into two equal parts that are equal up to rotation, the cut must be symmetric in some way. 

For a 2n × 2n square, the center is at position (n+0.5, n+0.5) if we consider coordinates from 1 to 2n.

The key insight is that for a square to be cut into two equal parts that are equal up to rotation, the cut must pass through the center of the square. This is because rotation symmetry requires that the two parts can be rotated into each other, and for a square, the only way this works is if the cut passes through the center.

Additionally, since the cut must avoid the marked cell, we need to check if there exists a path through the center that doesn't pass through the marked cell.

## Analysis of the Examples

Example 1: 4×4 square, marked cell at (1,1)
- The center is at (2.5, 2.5)
- We need to check if we can draw a path from one edge to another that passes through the center but doesn't go through (1,1)

Example 2: 2×2 square, marked cell at (2,2)
- The center is at (1.5, 1.5)
- We need to check if we can draw a path through the center avoiding (2,2)

## Critical Observation

For a square to be cut into two equal parts that are equal up to rotation, the cut must pass through the center. The most natural cuts are:
- Vertical cut through the center column
- Horizontal cut through the center row  
- Diagonal cuts through the center

However, since we need the parts to be equal up to rotation, any cut that goes through the center and doesn't touch the marked cell should work.

But is every cut through the center valid? Let's think about when a cut through the center would pass through the marked cell.

In a 2n × 2n grid with center at (n+0.5, n+0.5):
- A vertical cut at x = n+0.5 would pass through cells where x-coordinate = n+0.5
- A horizontal cut at y = n+0.5 would pass through cells where y-coordinate = n+0.5
- Diagonal cuts would pass through cells where the line passes through

But the marking is on a cell, not on a line. The cut goes along grid lines, so it only touches a cell if it passes through its boundary.

## Key Insight: When does a cut through the center pass through a cell?

For a vertical cut at x = n+0.5, it will pass through the boundaries between columns n and n+1. This cut will go through the left and right boundaries of columns n and n+1, but it won't pass through any single cell's interior.

Actually, the cut passes through the edges between cells. A cell at position (x,y) has boundaries at x-1, x, x+1 and y-1, y, y+1.

The cut through the center will pass through cells only if it intersects their boundaries. For a vertical cut at x = n+0.5, it will pass through the boundaries between column n and column n+1 for every row.

For a given cell (x,y), the cut passes through it if the cut intersects the cell's boundaries. 

But since we're making a broken line, we don't need a single straight line - we can make a zig-zag path that goes through the center and avoids the marked cell.

However, the problem is asking if such a cut exists.

```python
# Read input
line = input().split()
size = int(line[0])
x = int(line[1])
y = int(line[2])

# Calculate n
n = size // 2

# The critical cell is at (n+1, n+1)
if x == n + 1 and y == n + 1:
    print("NO")
else:
    print("YES")
```

## Key Insights

Since we need to cut the square into two equal parts that are equal up to rotation, we need to think about what symmetry this implies.

For a square to be cut into two equal parts that are rotationally equivalent, the cut must respect some symmetry of the square. Given that the parts must be equal up to rotation, the cut should generally pass through the center of the square.

Let me think about the constraints:
- The square has even side length 2n
- The marked cell cannot be on the cutting line
- The two resulting parts must be congruent through rotation

A key observation: if we can draw a line that cuts the square into two equal parts and the parts are rotationally equivalent, then the cut must be symmetric with respect to the center of the square.

Since we're dealing with a grid and need the parts to be equal up to rotation, the natural symmetry is rotational symmetry of 180 degrees. 

For two parts to be equal up to rotation, particularly 180-degree rotation, the cut line should pass through the center of the square, and any cell that is in one part after rotation should map to a cell in the other part.

A crucial insight: if the square has side 2n, then the center is at position (n+0.5, n+0.5) when rows and columns are numbered from 1 to 2n.

For the cut to work, the marked cell must not be on any potential cutting line. But since we can choose any broken line (as long as it goes along grid lines), we need to determine if there exists a valid cutting path.

Actually, let me reconsider. The key insight from similar problems: for a 2n×2n grid, we can always cut it into two equal parts that are equal up to rotation by drawing a line through the center, unless the marked cell is at a position that breaks the symmetry.

But more specifically: the marked cell should not be on any potential symmetry line.

After thinking about symmetry: if we have a 2n×2n grid, we can cut it through the center in various ways (horizontal, vertical, or diagonal). For the parts to be equal up to rotation, the cut needs to respect the center.

A more fundamental observation: in a square that can be divided into two equal parts that are rotationally equivalent, if we rotate 180 degrees, each cell (i,j) maps to cell (2n+1-i, 2n+1-j).

So the condition is: if the marked cell (x,y) is NOT equal to its 180-degree rotation counterpart (2n+1-x, 2n+1-y), then we might be able to make a cut that avoids it.

But if the marked cell is exactly at its 180-degree symmetric position, then it's on the center line. For even-sized squares, the center is between cells, so cells are paired through 180-degree rotation.

Let me work through the examples:

Example 1: 4×4, marked at (1,1)
- 180-degree rotation of (1,1) is (4,4)
- (1,1) ≠ (4,4), so it's not symmetric to itself
- So we should be able to make a cut

Example 2: 2×2, marked at (2,2)
- 180-degree rotation of (2,2) is (1,1) 
- (2,2) ≠ (1,1), so it's not symmetric to itself
- But answer is NO!

Actually, in a 2×2 grid:
- Cells: (1,1), (1,2), (2,1), (2,2)
- 180-degree rotation: (1,1)→(2,2), (1,2)→(2,1), (2,1)→(1,2), (2,2)→(1,1)

For any cell, its 180-degree rotation counterpart is (3-x, 3-y) since 2n=2, so 2n+1=3.

In example 2: (2,2) → (1,1), so (2,2) is not symmetric to itself.

But the answer is NO. So my theory is wrong.

Alternative approach: maybe the constraint is that for a cut to exist, the marked cell must not be on a line of symmetry that must be avoided.

Another insight: in a 2n×2n grid, one possible way to cut into two equal parts is to go through the center. The center is at (n+0.5, n+0.5).

For the cutting line to avoid the marked cell, the marked cell should not be on any line that would necessarily be part of the cutting path.

But we can choose any broken line. However, for the parts to be equal up to rotation, the cut must be symmetric under 180-degree rotation.

This means that if a cell is in one part, its 180-degree rotation must be in the other part (or in the same part if on the center line).

But since the cut is a continuous line, it needs to avoid the marked cell.

A key realization: in a 2n×2n grid, when we apply 180-degree rotation, cells are paired: (i,j) ↔ (2n+1-i, 2n+1-j).

The only cells that map to themselves are when i = 2n+1-i and j = 2n+1-j, which means i = (2n+1)/2 and j = (2n+1)/2.

Since 2n is even, (2n+1) is odd, so (2n+1)/2 is not an integer. Therefore, NO cell maps to itself under 180-degree rotation!

This means every cell has a distinct 180-degree rotation partner.

For the cut to divide the square into two equal parts that are equal up to rotation, the cutting line must separate cells in such a way that if cell A is in one part, then its 180-rotated counterpart is in the other part.

Since every cell has a unique rotation partner, the cut will naturally pair up cells.

The only constraint is that the marked cell cannot be on the cutting line.

For any valid cutting line that goes through the center and divides the square into two equal parts that are rotationally equivalent, it must pass through the center and avoid the marked cell.

Since the center is not at a grid point but between cells, a line can avoid any specific cell.

For a 2×2 grid, any cut through the center will divide it into two equal right-angled triangles or rectangles. But we need them to be equal up to rotation.

A vertical cut (column 1.5) gives two rectangles of 2×1 each. Rotating one 90 degrees gives a different shape (a 1×2 rectangle), which is not the same as the original 2×1 rectangle. 

A diagonal cut from (1,1) to (2,2) gives two triangles. Rotating one 180 degrees gives a different orientation, but are they the same?

But the problem says "equal up to rotation", so rotating one part should give the other.

For diagonal cut from (1,1) to (2,2), the two triangles are congruent by 180-degree rotation. Similarly, a cut from (1,2) to (2,1) would give two triangles that are also congruent by 180-degree rotation.

But if we cut vertically at column 1.5, we get two 2×1 rectangles. Rotating one by 90 degrees gives a 1×2 rectangle, which is different from 2×1. So they are not equal up to rotation.

Similarly for horizontal cut.

Therefore, the only cuts that give parts equal up to rotation are the diagonal cuts.

Is this considered a "common point" with the marked cell?

The problem says "common points with the marked cell". If the line passes through a vertex of the cell, does that count?

In grid problems, often vertices of cells are considered part of the cells.

The marked cell is defined as a cell, so its boundaries are part of it.

Therefore, if the line passes through any point of the marked cell (including vertices), it is not allowed.

The line from (1,2) to (2,1) passes through (1.5,1.5), which is the top-left corner of cell (2,2). Since cell (2,2) includes its boundaries, this point is part of the marked cell.

Therefore, the line passes through the marked cell, which is not allowed.

Similarly, any line that goes through the diagonally opposite corner or edge of the marked cell is invalid.

For a 2×2 grid, the only possible cuts that give parts equal up to rotation are the two diagonal cuts:
- From (1,1) to (2,2) - passes through (1,1), (1,2), (2,1), (2,2) at corners
- From (1,2) to (2,1) - passes through (1,2), (2,1), and (1.5,1.5) which is in cell (2,2)

Both diagonals pass through corners. The first cut from (1,1) to (2,2) passes through cell (2,2) at its bottom-right corner (point (2,2)).
The second cut from (1,2) to (2,1) passes through the top-left corner of cell (2,2) at (1.5,1.5).

So both cuts pass through cell (2,2) at some point.

In a 2×2 grid, every diagonal cut passes through the center and will pass through the corners of cells. Since cell (2,2) is one of the cells, and the diagonal cuts go through its corners, they will always pass through cell (2,2).

For any cell at position (i,j) in a 2n×2n grid, when we cut the square with a diagonal, the cut line may pass through that cell.

But in general, for a 2n×2n grid, the cut line will pass through a cell if the cell contains any point of the cut line.

For the cut to avoid the marked cell, we need that no part of the cut line lies within the marked cell.

For a diagonal cut from (1,1) to (2n,2n), it will pass through cells.
For a diagonal cut from (1,2n) to (2n,1), it will pass through cells.

But in general, for a 2n×2n grid, with n≥1, the only cuts that give parts equal up to rotation are the two diagonal cuts (and possibly others, but in square with rotational symmetry, diagonals are the only ones that give rotationally equivalent parts).

At (5-x,5-y): should satisfy the equation.

This is not necessarily symmetric.

The only symmetric lines are those that are either on the diagonals or have symmetry.

In 2n×2n, the only lines symmetric under 180-degree rotation that are straight lines might be the diagonals and the lines of constant (x+y) or (x-y), but transformed.

For example, a line of constant (x+y) is not symmetric under 180-degree rotation.

For example, x+y=3 in 4×4: points (1,2),(2,1)
Under 180: (1,2) -> (4,3), (2,1) -> (3,4)
(4,3) has x+y=7, (3,4) has x+y=7, not 3.

So not symmetric.

Only the diagonals are symmetric under 180-degree rotation.

Therefore, in 2n×2n, the only cuts that divide the square into two parts equal up to rotation are the two diagonal cuts: from (1,1) to (2n,2n) and from (1,2n) to (2n,1).

For the marked cell, if it is at a point that is on either of these diagonal cuts, then the cut passes through it, and it's not allowed.

So the cut is possible if and only if the marked cell is not on either diagonal.

For the main diagonal: x = y
For the anti-diagonal: x + y = 2n + 1

So in example 1: 4×4, (1,1)
- x=1, y=1: x=y, so on main diagonal -> should be NO, but answer is YES.

Contradiction.

Unless the diagonal cut from (1,4) to (4,1) is allowed, and for (1,1), it is not on that cut.

Check: on anti-diagonal: x+y = 1+1 = 2, 2n+1=5, 2 != 5, so not on anti-diagonal.

So (1,1) is not on either diagonal? 
- On main diagonal: x=y only if 1=1, yes, so it is on the main diagonal.

But in the example, the output is YES, which means a cut exists.

So either the cut is not only the diagonals, or the marking at (1,1) on the main diagonal is allowed if we choose the anti-diagonal.

The anti-diagonal from (1,4) to (4,1): does it pass through (1,1)?
- The anti-diagonal: x+y=5 for the line from (1,4) to (4,1) at (1,4):1+4=5, (4,1):4+1=5, so the line is x+y=5.
- At (1,1): 1+1=2 ≠ 5, so not on the line.

Therefore, for (1,1), we can use the cut from (1,4) to (4,1), which does not pass through (1,1).

Similarly, for (2,2) in 2×2: 
- Main diagonal: x=y, (2,2) has 2=2, so on main diagonal
- Anti-diagonal: x+y=4, 2n+1=3, 4≠3, so not on anti-diagonal
- So (2,2) is on the main diagonal, not on anti-diagonal.

For a 2×2 grid, the main diagonal cut from (1,1) to (2,2) passes through (2,2), which is the marked cell, so not allowed.
The anti-diagonal cut from (1,2) to (2,1) passes through (1.5,1.5), which is in cell (2,2), so not allowed.

Therefore, in 2×2, both diagonal cuts pass through the marked cell (2,2) at some point.

In 4×4, for (1,1), the main diagonal cut passes through (1,1), but the anti-diagonal cut does not.

So we can choose the anti-diagonal cut to avoid it.

For a cell (x,y) in 2n×2n, we can avoid it with a cut if there exists a diagonal cut that does not pass through it.

The main diagonal cut (x=y) passes through (x,y) if x=y.
The anti-diagonal cut (x+y=2n+1) passes through (x,y) if x+y=2n+1.

Therefore, if (x,y) is not on either diagonal, then both diagonal cuts avoid it, so we can choose one.

If (x,y) is on exactly one diagonal, then we can choose the other diagonal cut, which avoids it.

If (x,y) is on both diagonals, then it is on both, so no cut avoids it.

When is a cell on both diagonals?
- x = y and x+y = 2n+1
- From x=y, 2x = 2n+1, so x = (2n+1)/2
- Since 2n is even, 2n+1 is odd, so x is not integer.
- Therefore, no cell is on both diagonals.

Therefore, for any cell (x,y), it is on at most one diagonal.

Thus, if it is on one diagonal, we can choose the other diagonal cut to avoid it.

Only if it is on both, which never happens, so in all cases, we can choose a diagonal cut that avoids the marked cell.

But example 2 says NO for (2,2) in 2×2.

In 2×2, (2,2): x=2, y=2
- Main diagonal: x=y -> 2=2, true
- Anti-diagonal: x+y = 4, 2n+1=3, 4≠3, false
- So only on main diagonal.

Therefore, we should be able to use the anti-diagonal cut.

But earlier I thought the anti-diagonal cut from (1,2) to (2,1) passes through (1.5,1.5), which is in cell (2,2).

Is (1.5,1.5) in cell (2,2)?

Cell (2,2) occupies row from 1 to 2, column from 1 to 2.
Point (1.5,1.5) has row 1.5 (between 1 and 2), column 1.5 (between 1 and 2), so yes, it is in cell (2,2).

Therefore, the cut passes through the cell.

The issue is that the diagonal cut from (1,2) to (2,1) is a straight line, and it passes through the center of the square, which is in cell (2,2) if we consider the entire cell.

In a 2×2 grid, cell (2,2) is the bottom-right cell, and the point (1.5,1.5) is in the bottom-left corner of cell (2,2)? 

Let's define: cell (i,j) has corners at (i-0.5, j-0.5) to (i+0.5, j+0.5).

For cell (1,1): (0.5,0.5) to (1.5,1.5)
Cell (1,2): (0.5,1.5) to (1.5,2.5)
Cell (2,1): (1.5,0.5) to (2.5,1.5)
Cell (2,2): (1.5,1.5) to (2.5,2.5)

Point (1.5,1.5): is it in cell (2,2)? Cell (2,2) has row from 1.5 to 2.5, column from 1.5 to 2.5, so (1.5,1.5) is the top-left corner of cell (2,2), so yes, it is on the boundary.

Therefore, the cut line passes through the marked cell (2,2).

For any cell (i,j), if the cut line passes through any point of that cell, it is not allowed.

In the case of the anti-diagonal cut from (1,2) to (2,1): in general, it may pass through a cell.

For a cell (i,j) in 2n×2n, when does the anti-diagonal cut from (1,2n) to (2n,1) pass through it?

The anti-diagonal cut has equation x+y = 2n+1.

The cell (i,j) occupies x from i-0.5 to i+0.5, y from j-0.5 to j+0-0.5.

The cut passes through the cell if there is a point (x,y) on the line x+y=2n+1 with x in [i-0.5, i+0.5] and y in [j-0.5, j+0.5].

That is, if there exists x in [i-0.5, i+0.5] such that y = 2n+1-x is in [j-0.5, j+0.5].

So in 2×2, for cell (2,2): i=2, j=2
- x in [1.5,2.5]
- y = 3-x, so when x=1.5, y=1.5; x=2.5, y=0.5
- y must be in [1.5,2.5]
- when x=1.5, y=1.5 in [1.5,2.5] -> yes
- so at (1.5,1.5), which is in cell (2,2)

Therefore, for cell (2,2), the anti-diagonal cut passes through it.

Similarly, for cell (1,1): i=1, j=1
- x in [0.5,1.5]
- y = 3-x, when x=0.5, y=2.5; x=1.5, y=1.5
- y in [0.5,1.5] when y=1.5 (x=1.5) -> at (1.5,1.5), which is in cell (2,2), not in cell (1,1)
- y in [0.5,1.5] for x in [0.5,1.5]: when x=1.5, y=1.5; when x=0.5, y=2.5 >1.5, so only when y<=1.5, which is when x>=1.5
- x>=1.5 and x<=1.5, so only at x=1.5, y=1.5
- (1.5,1.5) is in cell (2,2), not in cell (1,1)
- cell (1,1) has y from 0.5 to 1.5, so at y=1.5, when x=1.5, point (1.5,1.5) is in cell (2,2) if row from 1.5 to 2.5, which it is.

So in 2×2, for any cell, the anti-diagonal cut passes through the center (1.5,1.5), which is in cell (2,2) only if that cell contains it.

In 2×2, (1.5,1.5) is in cell (2,2).

Similarly, in 4×4, (1.5,1.5) is in cell (2,2), not in cell (1,1).

For cell (1,1): row from 0.5 to 1.5, col from 0.5 to 1.5
Point (1.5,1.5) has row 1.5, which is the boundary between row 1 and 2, so in cell (2,2) as above.

Therefore, in 4×4, (1.5,1.5) is in cell (2,2), not in cell (1,1).

So for cell (1,1) in 4×4, the anti-diagonal cut from (1,4) to (4,1) passes through (1.5,1.5), which is in cell (2,2), not in cell (1,1), so it avoids cell (1,1).

For cell (2,2) in 4×4, does the anti-diagonal cut pass through it?
- Cell (2,2): row 1.5 to 2.5, col 1.5 to 2.5
- Line x+y=5
- When x=2.0, y=3.0 >2.5, too big
- When x=1.5, y=3.5 >2.5
- When x=2.5, y=2.5, which is on the boundary
- (2.5,2.5) is in cell (3,3), not in cell (2,2)
- when x=2.0, y=3.0, not in
- when x=1.5, y=3.5, not in
- when x=2.5, y=2.5, (2.5,2.5) is corner of cell (3,3)
- is there x in [1.5,2.5] such that y=5-x in [1.5,2.5]?
- y=5-x in [1.5,2.5] implies 5-x>=1.5 -> x<=3.5, and 5-x<=2.5 -> x>=2.5
- so x>=2.5 and x<=2.5, so x=2.5
- at x=2.5, y=2.5, point (2.5,2.5), which is the bottom-right corner of cell (2,2) (since cell (2,2) has row 1.5 to 2.5, so row 2.5 is the boundary)
- so (2.5,2.5) is in cell (2,2)

Therefore, the anti-diagonal cut passes through (2.5,2.5), which is in cell (2,2), so if marked at (2,2), it is not allowed.

In the example, for (2,2) in 2×2, it is not allowed, and in 4×4, (2,2) is also on the anti-diagonal cut at (2.5,2.5).

But is there a cut that avoids (2,2) in 4×4?

We only have the two diagonal cuts, and both pass through (2,2) at some point.

Main diagonal: from (1,1) to (4,4): at (2,2), which is in cell (2,2)
Anti-diagonal: at (2.5,2.5), which is in cell (2,2)

So both cuts pass through cell (2,2).

Therefore, for a cell (x,y), if it contains the center point (n+0.5, n+0.5) for 2n×2n, then every symmetric cut will pass through it.

The center point (n+0.5, n+0.5) is in cell (n+1, n+1) because cell (i,j) has row from i-0.5 to i+0.5, so when i-0.5 ≤ n+0.5 ≤ i+0.5, so i-0.5 ≤ n+0.5 and n+0.5 ≤ i+0.5
- n+0.5 ≤ i+0.5 -> i≥n
- i-0.5 ≤ n+0.5 -> i ≤ n+1
- so i = n or i = n+1
- for i=n: n-0.5 ≤ n+0.5 <= n+0.5, n+0.5 <= n+0.5, true, and n-0.5 <= n+0.5, true
- for i=n+1: (n+1)-0.5 = n+0.5, so n+0.5 ≤ n+0.5, and n+0.5 ≤ (n+1)+0.5, true
- so i can be n or n+1

Similarly for j.

So the center point (n+0.5, n+0.5) is in cell (i,j) only if i = n or n+1, and j = n or n+1.

In a 2n×2n grid, the center is in the cells: (n, n), (n, n+1), (n+1, n), (n+1, n+1)

For example, 4×4: n=2, center (2.5,2.5)
- cell (2,2): row 1.5 to 2.5, col 1.5 to 2.5 — includes (2.5,2.5)
- cell (2,3): row 1.5 to 2.5, col 2.5 to 3.5 — includes (2.5,2.5) at col 2.5
- cell (3,2): row 2.5 to 3.5, col 1.5 to 2.5 — includes (2.5,2.5) at row 2.5
- cell (3,3): row 2.5 to 3.5, col 2.5 to 3.5 — includes (2.5,2.5) at (2.5,2.5)

So only when i and j are in {n, n+1}.

For the cell (x,y), if it contains the center (n+0.5, n+0.5), then any cut that goes through the center will pass through it.

In a 2n×2n grid, the only cuts that give parts equal up to rotation are the two diagonal cuts, both of which pass through the center.

Therefore, if the marked cell contains the center, then every such cut will pass through it, so it is impossible to avoid.

Otherwise, if the marked cell does not contain the center, then we can avoid it by choosing a diagonal cut that does not pass through the cell.

For example, in 4×4, cell (1,1): contains the center? i=1, j=1, n=2, so n=2, n+1=3, so i=1 not in {2,3}, so not contain center. We can avoid by choosing the anti-diagonal cut.

In 2×2, cell (2,2): n=1, center at (1.5,1.5)
- i=2, j=2, n=1, n+1=2, so i=2 in {1,2}, j=2 in {1,2}, so yes, contains center.

Therefore, for cell (2,2) in 2×2, it contains the center, so we cannot avoid it.

For cell (1,1) in 2×2: i=1, j=1, n=1, so i in {1,2}, j in {1,2}, so contains center.

In 2×2, every cell contains the center (1.5,1.5) because for cell (1,1): row 0.5 to 1.5, which includes 1.5? 1.5 is the boundary, and if we include, then yes.

Cell (1,1): row from 0.5 to 1.5, so includes 1.5.
Cell (1,2): row 0.5 to 1.5, col 1.5 to 2.5, includes (1.5,1.5)
Cell (2,1): row 1.5 to 2.5, col 0.5 to 1.5, includes (1.5,1.5)
Cell (2,2): row 1.5 to 2.5, col 1.5 to 2.5, includes (1.5,1.5)

So in 2×2, every cell contains the center point (1.5,1.5).

Therefore, for any cell in 2×2, it contains the center, so any cut through the center will pass through it, so it is impossible to avoid.

For a 4×4 grid, only cells with i in {2,3} and j in {2,3} contain the center.

So for cell (x,y), if (x in {n, n+1} and y in {n, n+1} where n = 2n//2 = n), then it contains the center.

Otherwise, we can avoid it with a diagonal cut.

In the example: 4×4, (1,1): x=1, n=2, so 1 not in {2,3}, so avoidable -> YES
2×2, (2,2): n=1, x=2 in {1,2}, y=2 in {1,2}, so contains center -> impossible -> NO

Therefore, the algorithm is:

1. Read 2n, x, y
2. n = 2n // 2
3. if (x in {n, n+1} and y in {n, n+1}) then print "NO"
4. else print "YES"

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: 4 1 1
- 2n=4, so n=2
- x=1, y=1
- n=2, n+1=3
- x=1 not in {2,3}, so not in both, so "YES" -> matches

Example 2: 2 2 2
- 2n=2, n=1
- x=2, y=2
- n=1, n+1=2
- x=2 in {1,2}, y=2 in {1,2}, so both in -> "NO" -> matches

Another test: 4 2 2
- n=2, x=2, y=2
- x in {2,3}, y in {2,3} -> true -> "NO"

Another: 4 1 2
- x=1 not in {2,3}, so "YES"

This seems to work.

So the final solution is to check if the cell (x,y) is in the central 2x2 block of cells: specifically, if x is in {n, n+1} and y is in {n, n+1} where n = 2n//2.

Note: in 2n×2n, n = 2n // 2 = n.

So in code: n = size // 2

Final code:
```python
size, x, y = map(int, input().split())
n = size // 2
if x in [n, n+1] and y in [n, n+1]:
    print("NO")
else:
    print("YES")
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Little Petya loves playing with squares. Mum bought him a square 2n × 2n in size. Petya marked a cell inside the square and now he is solving the following task.

The task is to draw a broken line that would go along the grid lines and that would cut the square into two equal parts. The cutting line should not have any common points with the marked cell and the resulting two parts should be equal up to rotation.

Petya wants to determine whether it is possible to cut the square in the required manner given the sizes of the square side and the coordinates of the marked cell. Help him.

Input

The first line contains three space-separated integers 2n, x and y (2 ≤ 2n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ x, y ≤ 2n), representing the length of a square's side and the coordinates of the marked cell. It is guaranteed that 2n is even.

The coordinates of the marked cell are represented by a pair of numbers x y, where x represents the number of the row and y represents the number of the column. The rows and columns are numbered by consecutive integers from 1 to 2n. The rows are numbered from top to bottom and the columns are numbered from the left to the right.

Output

If the square is possible to cut, print "YES", otherwise print "NO" (without the quotes).

Examples

Input

4 1 1


Output

YES


Input

2 2 2


Output

NO

1. We have a 2n × 2n square grid

## Key Insights

Since we need to cut the square into two equal parts that are equal up to rotation, the cut must divide the square into two congruent regions.

Let me think about what types of cuts would work:

1. **Symmetry considerations**: For two parts to be equal up to rotation, there must be some symmetry in the square.

2. **Possible cut patterns**: 
   - A straight cut (horizontal or vertical) would divide the square into two rectangles
   - A more complex broken line might work, but the problem requires the parts to be equal up to rotation

3. **Rotation consideration**: If two parts are equal up to rotation, then if we can rotate one part to match the other, they are congruent.

4. **Critical observation**: In a square, if we can cut it into two equal parts that are congruent by rotation, then the cut must respect the symmetry of the square. This often means the cut must pass through the center point (n, n).

5. **Marked cell constraint**: The cut cannot touch the marked cell.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Little Petya loves playing with squares. Mum bought him a square 2n × 2n in size. Petya marked a cell inside the square and now he is solving the following task.

The task is to draw a broken line that would go along the grid lines and that would cut the square into two equal parts. The cutting line should not have any common points with the marked cell and the resulting two parts should be equal up to rotation.

Petya wants to determine whether it is possible to cut the square in the required manner given the sizes of the square side and the coordinates of the marked cell. Help him.

Input

The first line contains three space-separated integers 2n, x and y (2 ≤ 2n ≤ 100, 1 ≤ x, y ≤ 2n), representing the length of a square's side and the coordinates of the marked cell. It is guaranteed that 2n is even.

The coordinates of the marked cell are represented by a pair of numbers x y, where x represents the number of the row and y represents the number of the column. The rows and columns are numbered by consecutive integers from 1 to 2n. The rows are numbered from top to bottom and the columns are numbered from the left to the right.

Output

If the square is possible to cut, print "YES", otherwise print "NO" (without the quotes).

Examples

Input

4 1 1


Output

YES


Input

2 2 2


Output

NO

We have a 2n × 2n square grid, and we need to draw a broken line along the grid lines that:
1. Cuts the square into two equal parts
2. Has no common points with the marked cell
3. The two resulting parts are equal up to rotation

The key insight is that we need to divide the square into two equal areas, and since the total area is (2n)², each part should have area (2n)²/2 = 2n².
